
### Paper [1]
Title: Agentic-Teacher: An Agentic Data Engine for Failure-Driven VLA Post-Training
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17394
Authors: Gongyue Teng, Yuqing Xie, Yixian Zhang, Feng Gao, Shu'ang Yu, Jiaji Rao, Sukai Wang, Guanghui Ren, Ya Zhang, Maoqing Yao, Yu Wang, Chao Yu, Xinlei Chen
Abstract: Improving a pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) model requires demonstrations that address its weaknesses and provide effective supervision for learning. Coding agents can autonomously generate demonstrations through environment interaction, yet successful execution alone does not ensure effective supervision for VLA learning. We introduce Agentic-Teacher, an autonomous data engine for failure-driven VLA post-training. Given a VLA checkpoint, a task specification, and configured environment tools, the engine analyzes failed rollouts, selects intervention states, and iteratively develops closed-loop code policies through execution feedback. Validated policies become reusable demonstration generators that collect successful continuations from states where the VLA struggles. To make these demonstrations suitable for learning, Agentic-Teacher introduces temporally correlated action noise to broaden state-action coverage, removes idle frames while preserving gripper transitions, and filters trajectories using the frozen base model's flow-matching loss. The resulting demonstrations are combined with successful VLA rollouts for post-training, yielding an updated policy that operates without coding-agent assistance. Agentic-Teacher thus connects the weaknesses of a target VLA to both the generation and curation of its training data. In simulation, it improves $\pi_0$ success rates from 79.4% to 87.8% on LIBERO-10, from 80.4% to 90.6% on MetaWorld-10, and from 0% to 30.0% across three RoboCasa tasks.

### Paper [2]
Title: Racing the Clock: Teaching VLNs Budget-Conditioned Object Search
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17442
Authors: Maitree Meher, Siming He, S. Shankar Sastry, Jitendra Malik, Claire Tomlin, Jonas Frey
Abstract: A rescue robot entering a building with thirty seconds on the clock should sprint toward the single most promising room; given ten minutes, it should instead explore a broader tour. Yet vision-language-navigation (VLN) models are typically evaluated under a fixed episode limit that the policy never observes, even though time-critical missions require behavior to adapt to the available budget. We study budget-conditioned object search: given a target category and an explicit action budget, an agent must find as many distinct target instances as possible before the budget expires. We investigate how to supervise VLNs for this task by comparing a privileged TSP expert, which plans near-optimal routes using the full map and true target locations, with a room-level Bellman expert that maximizes expected return under partial observability using high-level cross-scene semantic priors. Despite the TSP expert achieving substantially higher expert performance, its demonstrations are less effective for learning exploration: on held-out HM3D scenes, the Bellman-supervised VLN consistently outperforms the TSP-supervised VLN, with the advantage becoming more pronounced at longer budgets. At 2000 steps (500m), the Bellman-supervised VLN finds 71.4% of reachable target instances, compared with 65.5% for the TSP-supervised VLN and 44.8% for the pretrained VLN. These results highlight a distinction between optimal routing and optimal exploration: effective exploration supervision should optimize decisions under partial observability rather than imitate routes that are optimal only once the environment is fully known.

### Paper [3]
Title: Learning Latent Goal Costs for Visual World-Model Planning with an Expanding Curriculum
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17529
Authors: Konstantinos Mylonas, Thrasyvoulos Spyropoulos
Abstract: Image-goal planning with a frozen visual world model depends on how predicted observations are scored against the goal. Mean-squared error (MSE) between embeddings from a pretrained visual encoder can provide a useful signal near a goal, yet favor states across an obstacle that are visually similar to the goal and difficult to reach. We fit a small residual adapter on top of frozen I-JEPA embeddings to improve this latent goal cost. Starting near each goal, a planner collects trajectories from progressively wider regions of initial states. The adapter learns from the order of observations along successful trajectories, with additional comparisons inferred from failed rollouts. No task-specific progress labels are used for fitting, so the procedure can be applied to other goal-reaching tasks. DotWall and PointMaze are used in this work. In paired 50-case evaluations with identical starts, goals, world models, and planner settings, success increases from 54% to 98% in DotWall and from 82% to 96% in PointMaze. On 16 unseen DotWall layout-goal cases, the adapter fitted on one fixed layout improves agreement between latent-cost ordering and shortest legal-path ordering from 71.9% to 85.8%. The results indicate that planning experience can improve a frozen visual representation's goal cost without retraining the encoder or the world model.

### Paper [4]
Title: Talk to Act: A Synthetic Multi-Turn Dialogue Benchmark for Human Intent Extraction and Action Planning in Robotics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17549
Authors: Deepu Krishnareddy, Mohammad Haziq Khan, Michael Danner, Matthias R\"atsch
Abstract: Multi-turn conversational human-robot interaction (HRI) continues to be a challenging problem in robotics, as many existing systems rely on rigid, command-driven interfaces that can be unintuitive for users. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) provide a promising direction, given their ability to process natural language, generate structured outputs, and support tool-based reasoning, all of which are relevant for interpreting user intent in robotic settings. However, progress in this area is constrained by the limited availability of datasets and benchmarks that capture the nuances of real-world human communication, such as multi-turn dialogue, ambiguity, and context-dependent requests.
  In this work, we present a synthetically generated multi-turn dialogue dataset for HRI, annotated with action intents, object references, and task plans across a range of scenarios and user personas. Our primary focus is on evaluating the capability of small, open-weight language models for robotic tasks, particularly in settings where local deployment and efficiency are important. To this end, we frame intent extraction as a tool-calling problem where each predicted action corresponds to a structured function call with arguments grounded in the dialogue and benchmark models ranging from 270M to 8B parameters on their ability to perform intent extraction and action planning without task-specific fine-tuning.
  Our results show that these models can achieve reasonable performance on the task even at relatively small scales and without any task-specific fine-tuning. This suggests that compact, locally deployable models already provide a useful baseline for conversational robotic systems. We expect that task-specific fine-tuning and more realistic training data will enhance performance further and make such models increasingly suitable for real-world robotic applications.

### Paper [5]
Title: Grounding Object-to-Object Hazard Reasoning into 3D Risk Fields for Task-Conditioned Safety
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17566
Authors: Mariana Sosa Guzm\'an, Yide Song, Amin Abyaneh, Gregory Dudek, Hsiu-Chin Lin
Abstract: Safety in robotics does not solely depend on geometric constraints, but also on semantics: the given task, environment properties, and interactions between objects in the scene. We present a framework based on vision-language models (VLMs) to infer task- and scene-dependent safety constraints for robot manipulation. We provide the VLM a structured safety tool based on an RGB-D observation and a natural-language task instruction that first decides whether the task should be rejected since its execution may result in a non-recoverable hazardous outcome. Otherwise, it identifies preventable object-pair hazards and specifies the spatial relations that must be avoided. Open-vocabulary segmentation and depth information are then used to ground the detected objects in 3D and construct continuous risk fields that represent the corresponding hazardous regions. These fields are integrated with an obstacle avoidance module that corrects nominal robot motions during execution while accounting for the risk field. We evaluate the framework on preventable hazards, tasks requiring rejection, and safe variants in simulation and the real-world. Project website: https://vlm-hazard-riskfield.github.io

### Paper [6]
Title: Natural Swing-Up of an Underpowered Double Pendulum
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17575
Authors: Arne Sachtler, Manuel Keppler, Florian Loeffl, Alin Albu-Sch\"affer
Abstract: Robots with weak motors must ride their natural dynamics rather than fight them. We swing up a direct-drive planar two-link robot, whose motors deliver only about 6\,\% of its shoulder gravity torque, along its nonlinear normal modes, two-dimensional invariant manifolds consisting of periodic orbits. On a non-strict mode, injecting energy necessarily moves the state off the manifold, so pumping and stabilization compete for the same bounded torque. We split the input torque into a power-neutral steering component and a scalar pumping input, the only component that changes the energy. The allocation then becomes a linear program in two variables: maximize pumping subject to the torque limits and one control-Lyapunov constraint. The constraint uses the model-based rate of the error, so no stabilizing feedback gain is needed. Experiments in hardware show swing-ups along both modes and compare the allocation with summing-and-clipping and with prioritized stabilization.

### Paper [7]
Title: X-Nav Technical Report: Real-World Long-Horizon Indoor-to-Outdoor Navigation on a Humanoid Robot
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17598
Authors: Jiaxin Huang, Chengxing Lin, Xiaoyu Luo, Sirui Chen, Jingyu Guo, Aolin Li, Kairui Chen, Rui Zhen, Cheng Wen, Kerui Gu, Shunyao Wang, Zhihui Peng, Shaoli Huang
Abstract: Long-horizon navigation from indoors to outdoors remains an open problem for humanoid robots: one instruction covers rooms, doors and roads, and the camera moves with the gait. Worken, AGIBOT's measure of productive robot work, depends on robots that reach their workplaces autonomously. We present X-Nav, to our knowledge the first system to take a bipedal humanoid through a language-instructed long-horizon mission from indoors to outdoors. X-Nav covers a pipeline from data to deployment. Its data engine collects navigation data in open-source scenes, in digital twins of deployment sites and on real robots. Its navigation model, MELIOR, takes only RGB images from the robot's built-in camera and comes in two versions: MELIOR-A predicts actions, and MELIOR-N predicts a navigation goal. Its agentic workflow, X-Navarch, turns a brief human instruction into sub-tasks and deploys MELIOR on the robot with explicit target navigation and obstacle avoidance. On the Lingxi X2, X-Nav completes a mission of about a hundred metres from an office lobby to a store in a neighbouring building; LiDAR and odometry are used for target approach and low-level control. The mission is completed with both versions of MELIOR: the robot follows a change of destination and walks through a door from the lobby to the road, and with X-Navarch it also passes static and dynamic obstacles on the route. During deployment, MELIOR is adapted with data collected in a digital twin of the site; the adaptation raises its closed-loop success rate in the twin, and the adapted model is then deployed on the robot at the real site. In addition, MELIOR achieves competitive results on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE benchmarks.

### Paper [8]
Title: TouchTwin: Scaling Human Touch Data via Real-to-Sim-to-Real
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17619
Authors: Huiwen Han, Nuo Chen, Ziyao Zeng, Dayou Li, Hao Wang, Amir Hossein Rahmati, Xiaoning Qian, Bangya Liu, Zhiwen Fan
Abstract: Human demonstrations are becoming the main source of data for dexterous manipulation, and real-to-sim-to-real pipelines multiply each demonstration in simulation. These pipelines rebuild the interaction from vision, which recovers how the hand moves. Which fingers touch, when, and how hard remain unobserved, because a hand pose shows where each finger stops on the surface rather than how far it is driven to close. This gap is a force gap: on held-out recordings, a hand policy that imitates the observed pose grips with 0.31x the recorded force, and a policy trained on physics replays of vision-tracked poses grips with 1.55x. TouchTwin closes the gap with measured human touch. Each selected TouchScale recording, captured with head and wrist RGB-D cameras and an 880-taxel glove, is rebuilt in MuJoCo. Coding agents write the objects as physics-tested programs, and a per-subject tactile hand replays the interaction with the recorded touch setting the contact and the closing force of each finger. In simulation, a policy trained on touch-grounded replays grips at 0.99x the recorded force and succeeds in 20/20 trials on held-out recordings and 35/36 unseen perturbations; a contact predictor trained on them misses 13% of real contact frames, against 32% for vision-based replays. Each replay also seeds perturbed demonstrations with simulated contact and force labels, rendered as realistic video in new views and scenes.

### Paper [9]
Title: On the Utility of Pre-Trained Visual Representations for Policy Learning of Contact-Rich Insertion Tasks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17631
Authors: Leon Kim, Dinesh Jayaraman, Michael Posa
Abstract: Visuomotor imitation learning has become a predominant paradigm for real-world robotic manipulation, yet it remains data-inefficient and brittle to task-irrelevant visual shifts. Pre-trained visual representations promise to ease this burden by distilling priors from internet-scale data, but the ``right'' representation for robotics remains unresolved: existing studies span broad but sparse task distributions and reach conclusions that vary and even conflict, pointing to strong task and setting dependence. We examine this question by bringing a magnifying glass to a common contact-rich manipulation task: insertion. We compare representations from widely used vision foundation models against an explicit contact-centric representation on two closely related insertion tasks, perturb each along controlled axes of occlusion and physical tolerance, and test whether conclusions transfer to the real world. Task dependence proves to be more subtle than previous, broadly scoped evaluations revealed: the best representation differs even between our near-identical tasks, and small changes shift trends in unpredictable ways. Despite this variability, results across simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate the distinct value of spatially-grounded, and in particular, contact-centric information for contact-rich policy learning, while underscoring how difficult it is to estimate such cues reliably under realistic visual complexity and sensor noise. We hope these findings motivate further study of representations in contact-rich manipulation, along with advances in contact perception and its integration into visuomotor policy learning.

### Paper [10]
Title: AI-guided robotic in situ fabrication of curvature- and tissue-adaptive wireless bioelectronics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17638
Authors: Hiroyuki Tetsuka, Jiaju Ma, Naomi Kumano, Minoru Hirano
Abstract: Personalized bioelectronic devices for wound therapy require not only conformal fabrication on complex body surfaces but also functional adaptation of their electrical characteristics to individual wound geometries. Here, we present an AI-guided robotic in situ printing platform for fabricating wireless wound electrotherapy devices directly on transparent wound dressings. The system integrates a 3D vision camera, a six-degree-of-freedom robotic arm, direct-ink-writing, and geometry-aware RF design optimization. AI-based wound segmentation is used to determine wound dimensions and local surface curvature, while the reconstructed three-dimensional geometry is incorporated into the electromagnetic design of the receiving coil and interdigitated electrodes. In contrast to conventional approaches that use surface geometry only for trajectory generation, the proposed method accounts for both anatomical curvature and tissue dielectric loading to compensate for geometry-induced shifts in resonant behavior. A printable silver ink was characterized in terms of rheology, dimensional reproducibility, electrical resistance, and electromechanical stability, and was used to fabricate multilayer wireless devices directly on curved polyurethane dressings. Wireless power transfer, impedance characteristics, and electric-field delivery were evaluated experimentally and numerically. Finally, robotic in situ fabrication was demonstrated on an anthropomorphic tissue-equivalent forearm phantom, where the measured dielectric properties of the phantom were incorporated into the RF optimization, enabling resonant operation near the target frequency of 25 MHz. This work establishes a perception-to-design-to-fabrication framework for geometry-adaptive wireless bioelectronics and provides a technological foundation for future physical-AI-enabled manufacturing of personalized on-body electrotherapy devices.

### Paper [11]
Title: Imitation Policies Learn a Proprioceptive Anchor: A Predictive Law for Perception Noise and Its Closed Loop Cost
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17658
Authors: Christopher Malili
Abstract: Imitation policies for robot manipulation are often trained on noisy object positions, and they learn to discount the noisy reading. We show how much they discount it, and why. Because demonstrators steer toward the object, the robot's own state carries information about where the object is. A trained policy combines this proprioceptive anchor with the reading and weights the reading by $\alpha = V/(V + s^2)$, where $s^2$ is the reading noise and $V$ the anchor's error variance. $V$ belongs to the learner and is far smaller than a linear model of the demonstrations implies, which is why classical errors-in-variables (EIV) regression overestimates $\alpha$. Measured once, $V$ predicted ten held-out sensitivities across two robomimic tasks, two learners and four new noise levels, with mean absolute error 0.025 against 0.37 for classical EIV, and a parameter-free bias test rules out the simpler explanation that the policies just overestimate the noise. The discount does not carry over to deployment, where the policy produces its own state: closed-loop sensitivity to a persistent reading offset exceeded $\alpha$ in all 15 policies that passed the registered gates, although the stricter registered prediction, that all would follow the offset fully, held for 8 of them. Post hoc, lower $\alpha$ went with lower success, steeply for diffusion, and a registered prospective test confirmed this for diffusion on Can at two new noise levels: the measured $\alpha$ predicted success before any rollout, within 0.03 (95% intervals $\pm 0.08$), although training noise predicts it equally well. Every test was registered before its data, including the classical benchmark that failed, and all other analyses are labelled post hoc; all results are in simulation with synthetic Gaussian noise.

### Paper [12]
Title: EViDS: An Environment--Vehicle Dynamics Integrated Simulation Platform for Intelligent Chassis Development
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17660
Authors: Lu Xiong, Yiming Zhou, Xuge Hu, Yi Zhang, Haoran Lv, Chen Tang
Abstract: The development of intelligent chassis increasingly requires the integration of environmental and perception information with high-fidelity vehicle dynamics to support environment-aware intelligent chassis control. However, existing autonomous-driving simulators and professional vehicle-dynamics tools are generally developed for different purposes, making it difficult to integrate environmental information, vehicle dynamics, and chassis algorithms. This paper proposes EViDS, an Environment-Vehicle Dynamics Integrated Simulation Platform for Intelligent Chassis Development. EViDS integrates CARLA-based environment and sensor simulation with CarSim-based high-fidelity vehicle dynamics. Reference-pose-based state alignment and multi-rate interaction maintain spatial and temporal consistency, while ray-casting-based tire-road interaction transfers environmental information to the vehicle-dynamics model. Scenario configuration, synchronized data acquisition, and algorithm interfaces further support intelligent chassis development. The platform is evaluated through environment-dynamics interaction tests with vehicle-dynamics consistency comparisons, together with intelligent chassis applications. Results show that EViDS preserves vehicle-dynamics characteristics, reliably transfers environmental information, and supports online closed-loop intelligent chassis control, providing a unified simulation basis for environment-aware intelligent chassis development and validation.

### Paper [13]
Title: Ego2Flow: Benchmarking the Traffic-System Impact of Autonomous Driving on Maps Distilled from Real-World Roads
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17720
Authors: Yu Dong, Weiying Shen, Hangyu Li, Shu Han, Hong Li, Xiangbo Gao, Keshu Wu, Zhengzhong Tu, Soyoung Ahn, Zhiyuan Liu, Xiaopeng Li
Abstract: End-to-end autonomous driving is predominantly evaluated through ego-vehicle safety and task completion. Its deployment in urban traffic, however, creates sustained interactions among vehicles whose collective effects remain insufficiently evaluated. We introduce Ego2Flow, a closed-loop benchmark for assessing the traffic-system impact of autonomous driving on maps distilled from real-world cities. To construct realistic 3D urban environments for sustained closed-loop traffic simulation, we develop an agent-guided map distillation framework that integrates real-world maps and observations with complementary geographic information. Through an iterative feedback mechanism and a traffic information graph that jointly guide scene generation, the framework preserves lane topology, critical road geometry and decision-relevant visual conditions while simplifying nonessential details and completing unobserved regions. Using our proposed framework, we generate and publicly release six CARLA maps from the Argoverse 2 dataset, covering the corresponding real-world regions and enabling paired comparisons of driving behavior under real and simulated observations. On these maps, Ego2Flow evaluates how autonomous driving policies affect traffic efficiency, safety, stability and energy consumption across diverse road structures. Compared with existing construction methods, our framework achieves the lowest average displacement error between planning outputs from paired real and simulated observations. We further benchmark representative driving policies to characterize their system-level impact on urban traffic. Project Page: https://github.com/Fisher-007/Ego2Flow.

### Paper [14]
Title: ERA: Error-Resistant Autonomous Object Navigation with Predicted Depth
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17730
Authors: Shunyang Huang, Shuo Wang, Wenyuan Xie, Mingyu Lin, Yue Ding, Hongtao Lu
Abstract: Depth and 3D foundation models can predict geometry from RGB images, allowing zero-shot object navigation without a depth sensor. However, when predicted depth is mapped as if it came from a sensor, view-dependent errors accumulate in the map, remove exploration goals, and end the search before the agent can observe and correct them. We propose ERA, a training-free object navigation system that replaces the depth sensor with depth predicted from RGB images and poses. ERA follows a paradigm we call Move with Error: movement provides new views that correct map errors, and the corrected map enables further movement. It realizes this paradigm with revisable occupancy and with exploration from reachable viewpoints, which continues the search when no frontier remains, and verifies the target across views before stopping. On three benchmarks, ERA achieves state-of-the-art performance among training-free methods without depth sensors. Its map and exploration components also raise VLFM's success from 10.8% to 61.7% under the same predicted depth. We further deploy ERA on a real robot.

### Paper [15]
Title: When Does Cross-View Consistency Matter in Multi-View Appearance Augmentation? A Controlled Study for Visuomotor Policies
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17751
Authors: Zixuan Jiang
Abstract: Multi-view visual augmentation often seeks consistency across cameras, but it is unclear which forms of consistency matter for downstream control. We use simulation-state replay to manipulate cross-view coupling while preserving the per-camera perturbation distributions, physical trajectories, and action labels. Across seven LIBERO manipulation tasks, marginal-matched changes in global appearance, ghost injection, and target color have small average costs for a late-fusion Diffusion Policy: point estimates are near zero or negative, and the largest upper endpoint of the 90% posterior credible intervals is 2.55 percentage points. In contrast, geometrically anti-coupled observations reduce success by 3.30 percentage points relative to coupled observations (90% CrI [0.64, 6.28]), with displacement magnitude, direction marginals, and the number of affected views matched. Camera interventions reveal substantial dependence on wrist-view geometry, while feature analysis shows appearance sensitivity patterns associated with the factors randomized during training. ACT shows a higher target-color cost estimate than DP, with uncertainty that includes zero and sensitivity to task composition. Within the tested tasks and policy configurations, these findings motivate selective consistency constraints: appearance coupling can sometimes be relaxed, whereas task-relevant geometry and cross-view spatial correspondence warrant protection.

### Paper [16]
Title: Beyond Prediction Gain: LASR as a Metric for Action-Grounded Latents in World Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17775
Authors: Yu Zhou, Changsong Lei, Yixiang Wei, Kairui Wen, Huankun Sheng, Ming Li, Yeying Fan, Yu-Hui Wen, Yong-Jin Liu
Abstract: World models that support planning, control, and interpretable reasoning require latent representations that genuinely encode action. Yet the large-scale video data from which such models are learned overwhelmingly lack action annotations. Latent-action models infer action-like variables from observation sequences, enabling action-aware learning from unlabeled videos or trajectories. A fundamental concern, however, remains: a latent that improves future prediction may simply encode the current state, episode progress, task identity, or other predictive shortcuts. Existing evaluation practices---reconstruction loss, rollout fidelity, action recovery, or downstream performance---cannot isolate this distinction. We introduce the Latent Action Support Ratio (LASR), a dimensionless metric that measures, under offline evaluation on held-out data, whether a learned latent provides predictive support for action-related targets beyond the current state and non-action alternatives. LASR provides two complementary summaries: GeoLASR captures typical support, while WorstLASR identifies the weakest case. We instantiate the LASR benchmark with recorded actions and corresponding physical state changes as targets, episode-held-out splits, one predictor type for all comparisons, and positive and negative controls. Experiments on recorded robot manipulation data and a matched multi-task panel demonstrate that LASR detects known action information, rejects representative non-action shortcuts, and enables graded comparisons among imperfect latent representations. We further use LASR to train and audit a lightweight residual adapter, obtaining descriptively better point estimates whose uncertainty is assessed with paired comparisons. LASR thus provides a principled offline protocol for measuring action-related predictive support in world-model latents.

### Paper [17]
Title: Multimodal Person Concept Model for Person Identification under Varying Observation Conditions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17802
Authors: Mizuho Okitani, Yoshinobu Hagiwara
Abstract: Service robots in domestic and office environments must identify users to provide personalized services based on their preferences and previous interactions. This is challenging because only a few observations may be available for learning, while observation conditions change owing to appearance, environmental noise, and utterance content. To address this challenge, this study proposes a multimodal person concept model that learns person concepts from a small number of observations, without person labels, and enables robust identification under changing conditions. The model employs a nonparametric Bayesian generative model based on the Chinese restaurant process and integrates visual, acoustic, and linguistic features extracted using Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP) and Contrastive Language-Audio Pretraining (CLAP), followed by principal component analysis (PCA). We evaluated six participants under eight conditions in which appearance changes, additive noise, and transcription errors were introduced individually and in combination. Averaged over the eight conditions, the proposed model achieved an identification accuracy of 0.89 while inferring the number of person concepts from the observations, which is comparable to k-means clustering that was supplied with the true number of participants (0.88). We also examined PCA dimensionality, modality availability, and variation strength, and demonstrated the model on a Toyota Human Support Robot. These results show that multimodal person concept learning supports robust person identification under changing conditions, and that identification can succeed even when the number of concepts is not correctly recovered. The project page and demonstration video are available at https://okitamisan01.github.io/person_concept/.

### Paper [18]
Title: Per-Agent-Safe Navigation of Globally Driven Robot Collectives via Affine Field Design
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17859
Authors: Seunghwan Jang, Steven Ceron, Wei Xiao
Abstract: Safe navigation of robot collectives requires coordinating motion and deformation so that the collective can traverse constrained environments while ensuring each robot's safety. Existing controllers for swarmalator collectives largely rely on local control, where each robot receives an individual command, which causes the control and communication requirements to grow with the number of agents. We introduce a broadcast control formulation in which a single spatially affine field governs both the motion and deformation of the collective. The scalar component of the field regulates collective size, and its spatial gradient generates collective translation through non-reciprocal interactions. Both components enter the collective dynamics directly, which allows per-robot wall, actuation, and cohesion constraints to be enforced using control barrier functions within a single quadratic program for the whole collective. The resulting controller continuously adapts the collective size to the local environment, and enables safe passage through corridors narrower than the unconstrained collective without pre-planned resizing. We further derive how the available actuation budget determines the reachable speed and minimum passable corridor width, and we demonstrate that two independently driven collectives can simultaneously traverse a shared junction using distinct fixed phase labels without inter-collective coordination.

### Paper [19]
Title: Reusing Locomotion Training Recipes Across Humanoids via Body-Aligned Virtual Joints
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18003
Authors: Sol Choi, Mingon Kim, Seokin Seo, Jongeun Choi, Donghyeon Kim, Yisoo Lee
Abstract: Reinforcement learning has enabled robust humanoid locomotion, yet training recipes (rewards, architectures, hyperparameters) remain hard to reuse across robots with different native joint coordinates, since policies output native joint angles while action scales, bounds, gains, and penalties are defined in those robot-specific coordinates. Existing transmission models convert native targets into actuator commands but do not standardize the coordinate interface exposed to the policy. We introduce a body-aligned virtual joint space upstream of this mapping: policy actions and observations follow a common anatomical convention, and a deterministic kinematic layer maps virtual targets to native ones, leaving the existing transmission and low-level control stack unchanged. Under a single unmodified recipe on three humanoids with different leg-axis layouts, it is the only interface that tracks accurately, keeps the feet aligned with the travel direction, and trains stably on all three, and on Unitree G1 hardware it reduces heading drift by nearly a factor of six.

### Paper [20]
Title: A Generalizable Approach to Safety-Aware Robotic Behavior Planning with an Explicit Constraint Space
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18026
Authors: Xianxu Wu, Shang-Ching Liu, Zhaocong Liang, Hengxiang Chen, Yujian Ma, Shenwen Deng, Qiang Li, Jianwei Zhang
Abstract: Large language and vision-language models have substantially advanced robotic behavior planning from natural-language instructions and visual observations. However, safe manipulation in tabletop environments remains challenging because the safety constraints required for a task depend on the surrounding scene and may not be explicitly specified in the instruction. We propose a safety-aware behavior planning framework based on an explicit Constraint Space that stores reusable textual safety rules. Rather than retrieving rules solely by semantic similarity, we formulate constraint selection as task- and environment-conditioned applicability learning. A lightweight query-conditioned dual-tower model identifies the constraints applicable to the current context, which are then provided together with the original task and image to a multimodal behavior planner. To handle incorrect constraint selections and previously unseen hazards, we introduce a multimodal Safety Inspector that evaluates generated primitive plans, reconstructs the complete applicable constraint set, and provides corrective planning feedback. Validated inspection feedback is further used for test-time adaptation of constraint applicability and parameter-efficient adaptation of the Safety Inspector through LoRA, while newly identified constraints are incorporated into the Constraint Space. The resulting framework integrates explicit safety knowledge representation,context-aware constraint selection, corrective safety inspection, and test-time knowledge adaptation for safety-aware robotic behavior planning.

### Paper [21]
Title: PoliMop: Policy-in-the-Loop Motion Planning in 3D Spaces for Mobile Robots
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18110
Authors: Yael Fassbind, Valentin N. Hartmann, Huang Su, Yijiang Huang, Stelian Coros
Abstract: Reaching a goal state in a complex environment often requires a robot to overcome challenging terrain and obstacles. Which routes are accessible depends on the robot's physical design, the control policy it uses, and how it modifies its surroundings. We present a kinodynamic rapidly-exploring random tree (RRT) planner that uses physics simulation to exploit skills encoded in the control policy, which allows it to find a path in the scene with respect to the robot's capabilities. The planner samples states from a reduced state space in 3D around the scene and expands the RRT toward each sample using policy rollouts. We guide sampling with a preplanned path for a simple mass-point model to focus exploration on promising regions leading toward the goal state, reducing planning time by up to 63%. Simulation experiments with quadrupeds, humanoids, and wheeled robots demonstrate that our approach supports drop-in integration of various robots and control policies, exploiting their physical and policy capabilities without modifying the underlying planning algorithm. Predefined environmental modifications, such as placing a plank across a gap to build a bridge, will automatically be picked up by the planner as the robot's reachable space expands.

### Paper [22]
Title: Evaluating Language Models as Spatial Priors for Object-Goal Navigation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18123
Authors: Jun Miura
Abstract: Semantic exploration methods for object-goal navigation use large language models (LLMs) to score how strongly a target object is associated with a room type or with a nearby object. This paper considers obtaining from the LLM a spatial prior instead: a distribution over the position of the target in a room. We evaluate whether two open-weight LLMs, Qwen3.5-27B and Gemma-4-31B-it, can provide such a prior. Using real room layouts from CubiCasa5K and ScanNet, we measure the likelihood the models assign to real and perturbed layouts, and the layouts they generate. The object frequencies and room sizes of the generated rooms follow the room type, but the proximity of the objects to the walls does not follow the differences between room types found in real rooms. The likelihood distinguishes real layouts from perturbed ones partly through their geometry and partly through the agreement between the coordinates and a field that states the wall each object touches; for the movable furniture of ScanNet, it is higher for layouts with all objects piled near the room center than for the real layouts in most rooms. When an object is given in the prompt, the models place a related object relative to it. When the wall of each object is given, they place most objects against that wall, but do not keep all objects inside a given room rectangle. As a prior for object search, the generated placements reduce the searched area relative to a uniform prior, but by less than a third of the reduction obtained with a prior built from real layouts, and by less than two thirds when both priors are spatially smoothed. These results support the use of LLMs for object-room associations and for writing coordinates under constraints determined by another component, followed by a geometric check, but not as a source of placement priors where real layouts are available.

### Paper [23]
Title: ESI: An Embodied Self-Improvement Flywheel for LLM-VLA Agents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18176
Authors: Yundi Cai, Zihan Wang, Chenxi Han, Yi Cheng, Houde Liu, Zilei Wang
Abstract: Independently pretrained language models and vision-language-action (VLA) policies provide complementary building blocks for embodied agents, but connecting them does not automatically produce coordinated behavior. The VLA may generate actions that diverge from the LLM's intended subtask and, upon regaining control after a correction, undo the promising state restored by the LLM. We introduce ESI (Embodied Self-Improvement), an automated flywheel that uses environmental interaction to align reasoning and action. ESI treats the modular agent as the unit of post-training, repeatedly refining the prompts that guide the LLM, the shared skill interface that expresses its intent, and the VLA parameters that realize that intent, then redeploying these components together. Our key insight is that collaboration itself can supply supervision for improving this interface. The LLM's subtask decisions provide semantic supervision for what the policy should execute, while its effective corrections and subsequent student continuations provide action-level supervision for recovery and handoff. The student also learns from its own effective execution, allowing locally useful experience to contribute even when the overall episode fails. On ten selected RoboDojo tasks, ESI achieves a mean Score of 62.03 and a mean success rate of 49.41% using an LLM-VLA agent without generating task-specific control code. On both metrics, it outperforms the published reference results of all hybrid embodied agents, including the Agent+WAM system GO-3 Nano Preview (47.27 Score and 36.60% success), as well as all compared code-based methods.

### Paper [24]
Title: NPU-Accelerated GNSS Signal Acquisition
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18186
Authors: Taro Suzuki
Abstract: On small single-board computers (SBCs), GNSS signal acquisition is a major CPU load. This paper proposes using the neural processing unit (NPU) of an SBC not for machine learning but as an accelerator for the parallel code search in GNSS signal acquisition. The discrete Fourier transform (DFT) used in acquisition is decomposed by the four-step DFT into matrix products of small DFTs and expressed by $1\times1$ convolutions, element-wise products, and transposes, which NPUs execute efficiently. Furthermore, we propose a method that divides the DFT, the non-coherent integration, and the peak detection between the CPU and the NPU according to the configuration of the NPU (an NPU with its own memory connected via PCIe, or an NPU sharing memory with the CPU). The proposed method is implemented in the open-source software GNSS receiver Pocket SDR and evaluated on a Raspberry Pi 5 combined with the external AX8850 NPU and on the NPU built into the SoC (RK3588) of the ROCK 5C, a small SBC of comparable size. Compared with acquisition by an optimized CPU implementation, the acquisition of short codes on the Raspberry Pi 5 with the AX8850 is 3.0-3.8 times faster. In the evaluation of the whole receiver, the number of tracking channels that reach their first lock within 30 s with the NPU is 1.5 times that with the CPU, and the time until 90% of the channels are locked is reduced from 66 s to 26 s. Furthermore, because the CPU load of acquisition is reduced, the number of tracking channels that can be processed in real time increases from about 175 to about 230. These results show that using the NPU on SBCs with limited CPU resources is effective for achieving both GNSS signal acquisition and tracking. The implementation is publicly available as open source under the name PocketSDR-NPU.

### Paper [25]
Title: Objectives as Policies: Vision-Language Model Cost Programs for Contact-Rich Manipulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18298
Authors: Zihao Lu, Trong Gia Hung Nguyen, Fuquan Wang, Yifan Zhu
Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have shown strong semantic and spatial reasoning capabilities in 3D environments. Recently, connecting VLMs with model-predictive controllers (MPC) has emerged as a paradigm for combining strong semantic reasoning with physics-based planning. However, connecting these two for contact-rich robotic manipulation requires a task representation that bridges the two and captures how physical interactions can be reasoned by VLMs. In this work, we introduce Objectives as Policies (OaP), a framework that connects a pretrained VLM to a shared sampling-based MPC through an editable objective program. Each program specifies a sequence of stages for completing a task, combining running and terminal costs from a cost library with stage completion conditions. Given a natural-language task instruction and scene observation, we build a simulation-based digital twin through real-to-sim, and the VLM generates an objective program that is executed by the MPC in the simulation. The program is iteratively updated by the VLM based on MPC rollouts, before being deployed in the real world. We evaluate our framework in both simulation and the real world on four tabletop manipulation tasks, and show that it outperforms strong baselines. Additional videos and examples are available on the project website: https://zihaolu001.github.io/oap-project/

### Paper [26]
Title: PROOF: Perceptive Robustness via Out-of-Distribution Fallback for Legged Locomotion
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18305
Authors: Siyuan Wang, Dongjie Zhu, Jinxuan Lu, Wenjun Xu, Jiankun Wang
Abstract: Vision-based perceptive locomotion has enabled legged robots to achieve agile and terrain-aware locomotion across complex environments. However, the reliability of such systems heavily depends on visual observations, which are inherently susceptible to occlusion, reflective surfaces, and illumination variations, often resulting in catastrophic locomotion failures under degraded perception conditions. In this paper, we present PROOF (Perceptive Robustness via Out-of-Distribution Fallback for Legged Locomotion), a perceptive locomotion framework that enables robust locomotion under severe visual disturbances. Our method adopts a two-stage teacher-student learning paradigm that explicitly models perception reliability: the teacher learns both perception-driven and proprioceptive fallback behaviors, while the student predicts exteroceptive features with uncertainty and suppresses them under out-of-distribution inputs. We evaluate our method on a Unitree Go2 quadruped equipped with a RealSense D435 depth camera in real-world settings. Under normal visual conditions, our method enables the robot to successfully perform high-dynamic parkour maneuvers, including traversing large gaps, elevated platforms, and narrow beam terrains. Under severe visual degradation, such as heavy occlusion and strong illumination changes, the robot continues to maintain stable and robust locomotion performance.

### Paper [27]
Title: Envisioning Agentic Robotics: A New Era of Robotic Systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18359
Authors: Grid Robotics,  :, Qingfeng Lan, Duo Li, Siyao Li, Yiheng Lyu, Shijian Lu, Zhenghao Qi, Shizun Wang, Xinchao Wang, Ming Yang, Zhiqin Yang, Zuhao Yang, Tao Yu
Abstract: The release of GPT-6 Astra marks the emergence of a new paradigm in robotics: agentic robotics. Within weeks, frontier models with agentic harnesses matched or exceeded task-specific policies on many embodied benchmarks. Instead of training a dedicated policy for each family of tasks, an agentic robotic system applies a foundation model to open-world embodied tasks. It operates in a closed loop: observing the environment, interpreting user intent, reasoning about the task, invoking skills and tools, verifying outcomes, and adapting its strategy. Despite these advances, the community has yet to converge on a shared definition, a reliable evaluation framework, or a research roadmap. We argue that Embodied System 2, realized by a foundation model together with an embodied harness, forms the decision-making core of agentic robotics. Its central challenge is to translate general-purpose reasoning into effective and verifiable actions under physical constraints such as limited reaction time, mechanical limits, unpredictable environment changes, and irreversible consequences. Motivated by this perspective, we propose an evaluation framework that measures both model capabilities and closed-loop system performance. Furthermore, we identify the principles that carry over from digital to physical agents and the challenges that must be addressed to achieve reliable physical autonomy. On top of the Embodied System 2 and the evaluation framework, we envision a research roadmap toward sustained autonomy: the ability to perform work safely and reliably with progressively broader responsibility, from executing individual primitives to fulfilling standing roles.

### Paper [28]
Title: Heavy Metal Acrobatics: Learning Dynamic Control for Skid-Steer Loaders
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18365
Authors: Pierre-Jean Lorentz, Pascal Egli, Edo Jelavic, Cliff Li, Ramitha Sundar, Harikrishnan Suresh, Paul Montgomery, Lee Redden, Matthias M\"uller, Ozan Sener, Vladlen Koltun, Marco Hutter
Abstract: Automation of heavy machinery offers the opportunity to improve productivity in mechanized industries such as construction, mining, and material handling. Yet, automation of such machines is challenged by their complex dynamics, in part because of hydraulic actuation that involves fluids, flexible elements, and nonlinear delays. A concomitant challenge is the size and weight of these machines, which can make experiments on the physical systems exceedingly dangerous. Here, we show that \ac{RL} in simulation, followed by transfer of the learned policies from simulation to reality, can successfully automate acrobatic maneuvers that push a heavy construction machine to the limits of its actuation and have only previously been accomplished by a small number of elite human operators in official world-record performances. We trained policies that perform autonomous acrobatic maneuvers on a John Deere 332G, an operational 5-tonne skid-steer loader. The policies can autonomously swing the machine up onto two wheels and, while balancing on two wheels, drive over rough terrain, trace out specified trajectories, spin around, and swing the bucket back and forth before landing it safely back onto four wheels. This work demonstrates that \ac{RL} and sim-to-real transfer enable dynamic autonomous control of heavy machinery, directly contributing to greater automation in heavy equipment sectors. Videos: https://sites.google.com/leggedrobotics.com/heavy-metal-acrobatics
  This is the author's version of the work. It is posted here by permission of the AAAS for personal use, not for redistribution. The definitive version was published in Science Robotics on 11 November 2026, DOI: https://doi.org/10.1126/scirobotics.aef0894

### Paper [29]
Title: Vision-Language-Action Models: A Life-Cycle Survey of Design, Training and Deployment
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18372
Authors: Suvarna Kadam (Witsense AI), Soran Parsa (University of Huddersfield)
Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models adapt web-pretrained vision-language models to output robot actions, so that web knowledge carries over to physical control. In under 4 years the field has moved from RT-2, the first VLA, to open models that fine-tune on a single consumer GPU and to proprietary generalists that clean unseen homes. This survey reviews 88 works from December 2022 to October 2026, mainly on manipulation and navigation, plus humanoid control, world-action models and evaluation. We follow a VLA through its life cycle, from model design to training and deployment, along seven axes. Model design covers (a) inputs and memory, what the model observes; (b) architecture, how its pipeline is built; (c) action output, how it produces motor commands; and (d) intermediate predictions, what else it predicts, from reasoning steps to future observations. Training covers (e) pretraining data, the mix of robot, web, human and simulated data, and (f) post-training, adaptation to the target robot. Deployment covers (g) real-time execution, keeping pace with the robot. We analyse 22 models on these axes and report their training data, model size, inference speed and hardware. Three trends emerge: (i) training data has broadened, from about 10,000 hours of teleoperated robot data ($\pi_0$) to 23,000 community-collected episodes (SmolVLA) and 20,000 hours of egocentric human video (GR00T N1.7); (ii) model sizes have split: most recent models have 3-7 billion parameters, while a new group under one billion runs on consumer GPUs; and (iii) generalist VLAs are emerging that handle both manipulation and navigation, such as UniVLA and Qwen-VLA. We also compare VLAs with world-action models, which predict future observations as well as actions. We review evidence that some VLAs with near-perfect benchmark scores fail when objects or the camera move slightly, and open up further research questions.

### Paper [30]
Title: The State Is Not the Bottleneck: A Paged-State Gated-DeltaNet Tile on a Tri-Mode Multicast Mesh, Bit-Exact from RTL to Silicon
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18422
Authors: Cong Xu, Ravi Sankar
Abstract: Linear-attention language models replace a growing key-value cache with a recurrent state of fixed size. On an edge FPGA, this state can exceed the on-chip memory and must be paged to external memory. This paper asks whether such paging dominates the cost of decoding (generating tokens). We built a hardware tile that computes one complete layer of a custom 369-million-parameter hybrid Gated DeltaNet model on an Agilex 5 FPGA and pages its recurrent state. The model state needs 6.2 MiB, whereas the device has 0.87 MiB of block memory. Paging adds 26.0 MB of memory traffic per decoded token, at most 11.2 percent of the calculated total. On the board at 75 MHz, with synthetic weights, one token takes 11.15 ms in this layer. The matrix-vector products with the weights take about 62 percent of this time and the state phase, including the paging, 13 percent. The tile is one node of a multicast mesh of tri-mode processing elements. We measure the error of the fixed-point arithmetic against floating point and check that the hardware agrees bit for bit with a fixed-point reference. In simulation, the state logic agrees with the reference on all 18 recurrent layers. On the board, six builds pass all 30 runs of 64 tokens and two runs of 1,024 tokens. For this model and memory interface, the delivery of the weights limits the decoding performance, and the recurrent state does not. State paging is therefore practical for such a model on an edge FPGA.

### Paper [31]
Title: From AI-Enabled Remote Patient Monitoring Toward Agentic Robotics: Embodiment, Governance, and Care Integration
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18484
Authors: Ivana Ragusa, Lorenzo Guiducci, Filippo Cavallo
Abstract: AI-enabled remote patient monitoring is evolving beyond passive data collection toward agentic AI systems that interpret patient states, coordinate care, and initiate action across domestic and clinical settings. This structured scoping review finds that mature agentic care has not yet emerged: Most systems remain human led, while stronger decision-action coupling and embodied AI remain largely confined to prototypes and pilots. Healthcare robotics makes the transition from monitoring to action especially visible by translating AI-generated recommendations and decisions into speech, movement, prompting, physical assistance, escalation, and care coordination, making embodiment, clinical integration, human oversight, and governance fundamental design requirements. Further progress requires safe and interoperable care architectures in which intelligent components operate through clinically validated and accountable workflows. We propose agentic robotics as a future direction for supervised systems embedded in the care ecosystem, connecting AI agents and robots with clinicians, caregivers, and patients through auditable workflows that can be interrupted and remain subject to human oversight and explicit AI governance.

### Paper [32]
Title: When Will You Hand It Over? A Hidden Start Delay Makes Self-Play Handover Robust to Unfamiliar Partners
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18491
Authors: Mohammad Khoshnazar, Mike Degany, Deyuan Qu, Qing Yang, Matyas Szanto
Abstract: Cooperative self-play can teach a humanoid to receive an object without any human data, but the partner it meets after training is never the one it trained with; we show that a fluent self-play handover policy breaks with unfamiliar partners, identify the main cause, and repair it. In a physics-based parcel handover between a Unitree G1 and a learned giver, both trained by alternating PPO self-play, the receiver completes 95% and 90% of handovers with its own partner (two training seeds) but only 31% with scripted partners it has never met. Paired interventions isolate the cause: under a shared reward the learned giver always starts its offer at once, so the receiver learns a single rendezvous, and delaying its own partner by one second cuts success from 95% to 6% (87% to 34% in the second seed). We introduce hidden start-delay randomisation (HSD), a one-line rule on the learned partner that the receiver never observes. After 300 further updates, success with unseen scripted partners rises from 31% to 80% in both seeds, against 35--41% for a budget-matched control. The gain holds on a fresh scenario seed, on eleven held-out partner conditions and on partners that start later than any training delay; it cuts high-force contact three- to five-fold and needs no retraining of the partner.

### Paper [33]
Title: Toward Resilient Self-Driving Laboratories through Telerobotic Interventions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18496
Authors: Ziheng Wu, Adrian Vecina Tercero, Andre A. Fatehi, Abhik Sarkar, Brian Giera, Eric B. Duoss, Christopher M. Spadaccini, Nikhil Deshpande, Aldair E. Gongora
Abstract: Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) accelerate scientific research by integrating robotics, automated experimentation, and machine learning to execute experiments autonomously. Biology and chemistry have adopted these systems. However, increasing SDL complexity heightens susceptibility to unexpected faults in automated workflows, highlighting the need for efficient recovery mechanisms. In this study, we investigate telerobotic interventions to enhance SDL resilience against unexpected failures. We developed a teleoperation interface enabling human operators to resolve disruptions during automated tasks. The interface enables precise control of a six-degree-of-freedom (DOF) robotic arm (UR3e) using a 3D mouse (3Dconnexion SpaceMouse Pro). Two velocity-based controller strategies were evaluated: (i) a coupled mode mapping all six DOFs to the 3D mouse's central knob and (ii) a decoupled mode separating translational and rotational motions. To evaluate the framework, teleoperators intervened across three SDL fault scenarios. In the first study, comparative trials showed that the decoupled controller was associated with shorter completion times and reduced rotational motion. In a second study, teleoperators performed an aiming task with and without reference-path guidance. Users were more target-driven and risk-tolerant without guidance but showed behavioral differences under different guidance strategies. These findings suggest that controller configuration and reference-path design influence operator performance during SDL fault recovery, informing teleoperation interface optimization. This work demonstrates the potential feasibility of teleoperation as a human-in-the-loop recovery mechanism for SDL workflows, combining robotic precision with human adaptability and problem-solving. These findings also provide a foundation for future studies in shared autonomy and semi-autonomous intervention.

### Paper [34]
Title: In Situ Monitoring and Predictive Modeling of Harmful Algal Blooms with Autonomous Surface Vehicles
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18501
Authors: Nenyi Kweku Nkensen Dadson, Jean Paul Gonzalez, Daniel P. Arabie, Ryan J. Cedel, Dorin Boldor, Corina Barbalata
Abstract: Harmful Algal Blooms (HABs) pose significant environmental, economic, and public-health challenges in coastal and inland water bodies, creating a need for scalable methods to collect spatially distributed water-quality data. This paper presents an autonomous surface vessel-based sensing system for in-situ water-quality monitoring related to harmful algal bloom detection. The system integrates a BlueBoat Autonomous Surface Vessel (ASV), a motorized winch for depth-controlled sonde deployment, a multiparameter water-quality sonde, side-scan sonar, and an event-camera payload. Autonomous waypoint missions were used to collect measurements across selected sampling regions and multiple depths, enabling both horizontal and vertical characterization of environmental conditions. Field deployments were conducted in Lake Pontchartrain, Louisiana, with additional measurements collected in False River, Louisiana to increase dataset diversity. The collected measurements included algal pigment indicators, nutrient concentration, turbidity, temperature, and other water-quality parameters relevant to bloom formation. In addition, complementary sonar and event-camera data were collected during vehicle operation to provide contextual environmental information. A machine-learning classification approach was also evaluated using the collected water-quality measurements to support HAB and non-HAB classification. The results demonstrate the feasibility of using a modular ASV platform for repeatable, multi-modal water-quality data collection and support future development of autonomous monitoring systems.

### Paper [35]
Title: Disturbance-Aware Informative Planning in Spatiotemporal Environments
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18505
Authors: Kaleb Ben Naveed, Haejoon Lee, Anouck Girard, Dimitra Panagou
Abstract: Autonomous robots are widely used for persistent monitoring of spatiotemporal environments, where quantities of interest (QoI) change across space and time and require repeated measurements. In practice, robot motion can be affected by external disturbances that vary across the environment. When such external disturbances are not considered in trajectory optimization, it can lead to inaccurate predictions of robot motion and, consequently, the information acquired about the QoI during execution. However, estimating disturbances throughout the environment can be inefficient, as disturbances in regions with little overlap with monitoring trajectories have limited impact on monitoring performance. We therefore propose a two-stage informative planning framework for unknown, spatially varying but time-invariant disturbances, combining informed disturbance sampling with disturbance-aware trajectory optimization. First, we identify where QoI measurements are needed and generate alternative paths to these locations using the environment geometry. We then prioritize disturbance measurements based on their expected reduction of disturbance uncertainty along these paths. Second, rather than optimizing information acquisition based on nominal robot motion, we incorporate the learned disturbance belief into trajectory optimization to account for its effects on robot motion and information acquisition. Across four simulation scenarios, the proposed method reduces disturbance-learning time by 25.0--52.3 percent compared with environment-wide estimation and identifies paths that appear costly under nominal dynamics but become preferable when disturbances are considered.

### Paper [36]
Title: Modeling the Frictional Contact of Continuum Robots with Environments through Linear Complementarity Problem
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18546
Authors: Jia Shen, Junhyoung Ha, Yue Chen
Abstract: Accurate friction modeling in continuum robots is essential for precise motion control and path planning. However, current approaches make predictions only for the mechanism's internal friction that is limited by the boundary forces of the Coulomb friction cone, which fail to characterize the static friction and motion hysteresis. In this paper, we propose a novel friction model based on complementarity constraints under the Coulomb friction assumption. This model predicts various friction effects caused by environmental interactions, including both static and sliding frictions and hysteresis behavior, as well as the corresponding robot deformation induced by these interactions, which are crucial for the practical motion planning and control of continuum robots. Additionally, real-time implementation of this model can be achieved, meeting the demand for computational efficiency in robotic applications. Experimental validations demonstrated that this model is able to reduce the maximum tip position error from 45.4 mm in the frictionless model to 5.85 mm in our friction-included model with a 287.9 mm-long tendon-driven continuum robot.

### Paper [37]
Title: Training Reasoning Models in Simulation to Natively Use Real Robots
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18562
Authors: Jiaming Tang, Zhuoyang Zhang, Junxian Guo, Fangzhou Zhao, Yilong Zhao, Zekai Li, Xingyang Li, Yuyang Zhao, Song Bian, Zhijian Liu, Song Han
Abstract: Robot-use agents (RUAs) have emerged as a promising approach to general-purpose robot control. However, strong performance has so far been achieved only by frontier proprietary models such as GPT-6 Astra and Claude Fable 5.2, whereas open-weight models remain substantially less capable. We show that an open-weight 27B reasoning model can be trained to use real robots natively through reinforcement learning, entirely in simulation and without demonstrations. Like a computer-use agent (CUA), the model observes the environment, reasons in a chain of thought, and generates the next end-effector pose, without any harness. We build a Post-training Engine for Agentic Robotics (PEAR) and use it to train the model on simulated robot tasks at scale with robot-use reinforcement learning (RURL), mirroring how frontier models acquire computer use. We find that the skills acquired in simulation generalize well beyond the training tasks: they transfer to an unseen robot, to the real world, and to novel instructions. After training on LIBERO and RoboDojo in simulation, the model succeeds in 60% of our trials on a real dual-arm robot, whereas the base model succeeds in none. On LIBERO, training raises the success rate from 1.6% to 74.4%, approaching GPT-6 Astra and GPT-6.1 Sol at maximum reasoning effort. Compared with $\pi_{0.5}$, a vision-language-action model fine-tuned on demonstrations, our model is substantially more robust to changes in the task itself. On LIBERO-Pro, $\pi_{0.5}$ succeeds in only 0.75% of episodes when assigned a new task in a familiar scene whereas our model succeeds in 57.4%. Finally, the model retains its general capabilities, with no measurable degradation on nine reasoning, coding and vision benchmarks. These results highlight robot-use reinforcement learning as a promising component of foundation-model post-training.

### Paper [38]
Title: CS-MPC: Contraction-Certified Sampling-Based MPC for Nonlinear Systems Under Uncertainty
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18390
Authors: Yashwanth Kumar Nakka
Abstract: We present contraction-certified sampling-based model predictive control (CS-MPC) for tracking and safety of nonlinear robotic systems under parametric uncertainty, disturbances, and safety constraints that are revealed online. CS-MPC uses a contraction-certified tracking feedback inside every sampled rollout, which concentrates the rollouts around the reference, and an execution layer that verifies the applied input using a quantile-tightened discrete-time control barrier function condition. We derive a verification procedure for nonlinear contraction of the feedback-closed dynamics, and we prove a finite-sample stochastic stability bound and a mission-level probabilistic safety guarantee. We validate CS-MPC in simulation on a rover, a quadrotor, and an asteroid landing. When compared with standard model predictive path integral (MPPI) control, CS-MPC attains the same tracking error with 7 to 20 times fewer rollouts for the rover, and a 2 to 4 times lower tracking error for the quadrotor, with no safety violations in 200 Monte Carlo missions.

### Paper [39]
Title: Grid-based Submap Joining: An Efficient Algorithm for Simultaneously Optimizing Global Occupancy Map and Local Submap Frames
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.12764
Authors: Yingyu Wang, Liang Zhao, Shoudong Huang
Abstract: Optimizing robot poses and the map simultaneously has been shown to provide more accurate SLAM results. However, for non-feature based SLAM approaches, directly optimizing all the robot poses and the whole map will greatly increase the computational cost, making SLAM problems difficult to solve in large-scale environments. To solve the 2D non-feature based SLAM problem in large-scale environments more accurately and efficiently, we propose the grid-based submap joining method. Specifically, we first formulate the 2D grid-based submap joining problem as a non-linear least squares (NLLS) form to optimize the global occupancy map and local submap frames simultaneously. We then prove that in solving the NLLS problem using Gauss-Newton (GN) method, the increments of the poses in each iteration are independent of the occupancy values of the global occupancy map. Based on this property, we propose a poseonly GN algorithm equivalent to full GN method to solve the NLLS problem. The proposed submap joining algorithm is very efficient due to the independent property and the pose-only solution. Evaluations using simulations and publicly available practical 2D laser datasets confirm the outperformance of our proposed method compared to the state-of-the-art methods in terms of efficiency and accuracy, as well as the ability to solve the grid-based SLAM problem in very large-scale environments.

### Paper [40]
Title: Foundation Model Driven Robotics: A Comprehensive Review
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.10087
Authors: Muhammad Tayyab Khan, Ammar Waheed, Seung Ki Moon
Abstract: The rapid emergence of foundation models, particularly large language models and vision-language models, has introduced a transformative paradigm in robotics. The models offer powerful capabilities in semantic understanding, high-level reasoning, and cross-modal generalization, enabling significant advances in perception, planning, control, and human-robot interaction. This critical review provides a structured synthesis of recent developments and organizes applications into four directions: simulation-driven robotics, open-world operation, sim-to-real transfer, and adaptable robotic systems. Unlike existing surveys that emphasize isolated capabilities, this work highlights integrated, system-level strategies and evaluates their practical feasibility in real-world environments. Key enabling trends such as procedural scene generation, policy generalization, and multimodal reasoning are discussed alongside core bottlenecks, including limited embodiment, lack of multimodal data, safety risks, and computational constraints. Through this lens, this paper identifies both the architectural strengths and critical limitations of foundation model-based robotics, highlighting open challenges in real-time operation, grounding, resilience, and trust. The review concludes with a roadmap of proposed future research directions for bridging semantic reasoning and physical intelligence through more robust, interpretable, and embodied models.

### Paper [41]
Title: A Stochastic Framework for Continuous-Time State Estimation of Continuum Robots
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.01381
Authors: Spencer Teetaert, Sven Lilge, Jessica Burgner-Kahrs, Timothy D. Barfoot
Abstract: State estimation techniques for continuum robots (CRs) typically involve using computationally complex dynamic models, simplistic shape approximations, or are limited to quasi-static methods. These approaches can be sensitive to unmodeled disturbances acting on the robot. Inspired by a factor-graph optimization paradigm, this work introduces a continuous-time stochastic state estimation framework for continuum robots. We introduce factors based on continuous-time kinematics that are corrupted by a white noise Gaussian process (GP). By using a simple robot model paired with high-rate sensing, we show adaptability to unmodeled external forces and data dropout. The result contains an estimate of the mean and covariance for the robot's pose, velocity, and strain, each of which can be interpolated continuously in both space and time. This same interpolation scheme can be used during estimation, allowing for inclusion of measurements that are asynchronous to the estimated states. Our method's inherent sparsity leads to a linear solve complexity with respect to time and interpolation queries in constant time. We demonstrate our method on a CR with gyroscope and pose sensors, highlighting its versatility in real-world systems.

### Paper [42]
Title: STITCHER: Constrained Trajectory Planning in Complex Environments with Real-Time Motion Primitive Search
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.14893
Authors: Helene J. Levy, Brett T. Lopez
Abstract: Autonomous high-speed navigation through large, complex environments requires real-time generation of agile trajectories that are dynamically feasible, collision-free, and satisfy state or actuator constraints. Modern trajectory planning techniques primarily use numerical optimization, as they enable the systematic computation of high-quality, expressive trajectories that satisfy various constraints. However, stringent requirements on computation time and the risk of numerical instability can limit the use of optimization-based planners in safety-critical scenarios. This work presents an optimization-free planning framework called STITCHER that stitches short trajectory segments together with graph search to compute long-range, expressive, and near-optimal trajectories in real-time. STITCHER outperforms modern optimization-based planners through our innovative planning architecture and several algorithmic developments that make real-time planning possible. Extensive simulation testing is performed to analyze the algorithmic components that make up STITCHER, along with a thorough comparison with three state-of-the-art optimization planners. Simulation tests show that safe trajectories can be created within a few milliseconds for paths that span the entirety of two 50 m x 50 m environments. Hardware tests with a custom quadrotor verify that STITCHER can produce trackable paths in real-time while respecting nonconvex constraints, such as limits on tilt angle and motor forces, with flight speeds up to 63 km/h.

### Paper [43]
Title: E0: Expressive Fine-Grained Discrete Action Prediction for Vision-Language-Action Models via Tweedie Discrete Diffusion
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21542
Authors: Zhihao Zhan, Jiaying Zhou, Likui Zhang, Qinhan Lyu, Hao Liu, Weizheng Li, Jusheng Zhang, Ziliang Chen, Tianshui Chen, Ruifeng Zhai, Keze Wang, Liang Lin, Guangrun Wang
Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models offer a unified framework for robotic manipulation by integrating visual perception, language understanding, and control generation. However, existing VLA systems still struggle to generalize across diverse tasks, scenes, and camera viewpoints, and often produce coarse or unstable actions. We argue that these limitations are closely tied to the structural properties of actions in VLA settings, including the inherent multi-peaked nature of action distributions, the token-based symbolic reasoning of pretrained VLM/VLA backbones, and the effective finite resolution imposed by real-world robotic control. Motivated by these properties, we introduce E0, a tweedie discrete diffusion framework that formulates action generation as iterative denoising over quantized action tokens. By operating in a discrete action space with a principled diffusion process, E0 naturally aligns with token-based reasoning, supports fine-grained yet executable action control, and avoids the distributional mismatch of masking-based discrete diffusion. Experiments on LIBERO, VLABench, ManiSkill, and a real-world Franka arm demonstrate that E0 achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse environments, outperforming strong baselines by 8.1% on average.

### Paper [44]
Title: Robots that Collaborate: Sequential Asymmetric Imitation for Learning Coupled Robot Policies
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16490
Authors: Ranpeng Qiu, Yincong Chen, Zihao Li, Yanan Zhou, Guoqiang Ren, Weiming Zhi
Abstract: Collaborative mobile manipulation requires robots to coordinate with a partially observed partner while physically interacting through shared objects. This is difficult because failures often arise not from poor local skills, but from mistimed waiting, yielding, pulling, releasing, or repositioning. We study this problem with two bimanual mobile manipulators coupled through rigid and deformable objects. We propose Sequential Asymmetric Imitation (SAI), a single-teleoperator curriculum for learning coupled multi-robot behaviors without synchronized dual-operator demonstrations or explicit inter-robot communication. SAI trains Robot A from unilateral demonstrations with a compliant human partner, trains Robot B against the deployed Robot A policy, and then refines Robot A using sparse interventions near coordination failures. This staged process exposes the policies to increasingly realistic partner behaviors, including delay, phase mismatch,insufficient yielding, and interaction conflict. Across real-world dual-robot manipulation tasks, SAI improves task success, phase synchronization, and partner-contingent yielding over independent imitation and curriculum-ablation baselines. These results suggest that physically coupled collaboration can be learned through the structure of the imitation curriculum, rather than through synchronized multi-operator demonstrations or explicit coordination mechanisms. More videos on project page:http://cyc0429.github.io/sai-project-page/

### Paper [45]
Title: Long-Distance Real-World Navigation of the Legged-Wheeled Robot Go2-W Using Deep Reinforcement Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21387
Authors: Takaaki Matsuzawa, Kiyoshi Irie, Tomoaki Yoshida, Taro Suzuki, Yoshitaka Hara, Masahiro Tomono
Abstract: Legged-wheeled robots have been extensively studied for their potential to combine the efficient flat-ground mobility of wheels with the rough-terrain adaptability of legs. However, their application to long-distance autonomous navigation in real environments remains limited. This paper reports our efforts to build a deep reinforcement learning-based locomotion controller and an autonomous navigation system for the commercially available Go2-W legged-wheeled robot. The study focuses on the integrated system and the practical challenges associated with its sustained operation over long distances in real-world environments. For locomotion control, we extended our proprioception-only policy, previously developed for quadruped robots, to Go2-W, a 16-degree-of-freedom legged-wheeled robot. We additionally found that wheeled locomotion concentrates the load on the hip joints, resulting in heat concentration that hinders sustained travel. To address this issue, we developed a policy with periodic foot-lifting motion that distributes the joint loads and mitigates heat concentration. We evaluated the integrated system at the Tsukuba Challenge 2025. The robot autonomously traversed an approximately \SI{2.8}{\kilo\meter} route that included sidewalks, a park, and stairs without stopping owing to overheating.

### Paper [46]
Title: Courteous Anticipation: Improving Long-Lived Task Planning in Persistent Shared Environments
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20289
Authors: Md Ridwan Hossain Talukder, Elizabeth Phillips, Gregory J. Stein
Abstract: We consider a task planning scenario in which robots sharing a persistent environment are assigned tasks one at a time from a held-out sequence. Standard task planners, lacking foresight of future tasks and inconsiderate of others' constraints, solve each task in isolation, leaving terminal states that increase future cost for all; side effects that compound over lengthy task sequences.
