To put this idea into practice, we connect work on automation and human-computer interaction to AI agent processes, outlining design-level affordances and organizational protocols that (1) support overseers in exercising critical judgement and (2) counteract the skill atrophy that arises from extended use of automation. We urge developers and deployers to adopt these or similar approaches. Without explicit support for the cognitive demands of effective human-agent interaction, AI agent systems will continue to passively incentivize the degradation of the very human skills they rely on.

### Paper [233]
Title: Efficient Test-Time Adaptation through Human-Agent Interaction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04141
Authors: Zora Zhiruo Wang, Apurva Gandhi, Rulin Shao, Aspen Chen, Jonas Mueller, Zhiqi Liang, Jett Chen, Michael Ryan, Qianou Ma, Luxi He, Zhoujun Cheng, Andre He, Seungone Kim, Jiayi Geng, Mingqian Zheng, Weiwei Sun, Zheyuan Zhang, Xinran Zhao, Yike Wang, Abe Hou, Liwei Jiang, Pang Wei Koh, Diyi Yang, Graham Neubig, Daniel Fried
Abstract: AI agents are trained on population-scale data to encode broad capabilities spanning those of many practitioners. Yet the artifacts they produce rarely meet the personal bar professionals need to stake their reputation on. On realistic, open-ended tasks where success criteria are heterogeneous and insufficiently documented, individual expertise lives precisely in the elevation and departure from the average. In practice, iterative human-agent interaction surfaces criteria that users cannot fully specify up front, yet apply repeatedly across tasks. We argue this cross-session interaction data is a rich, underused signal for closing the gap to individual expertise. In this work, we propose test-time adaptation through human-agent interaction (TAHI), which integrates these signals into agent context and weights, and crystallizes each user's training and evaluation criteria via an evolving rubric module. We adapt agents to 30 individuals in two high-utility domains, writing and visual creation, on a total of 600 tasks. Our agents improve solo task success by 4.5-20.9% within only tens of tasks. Meanwhile, our evolving rubric module serves as a scalable annotation tool, creating evaluation rubrics that catch 16.0-22.3% more failures than those from LMs or humans alone. While agents are adapted towards individuals, we show these personalized agents also produce improvements in success of up to 8.8% that generalize across users.

### Paper [234]
Title: MeanFlowAdvantage: Stable Reward Fine-Tuning for Few-Step Average-Velocity Generators
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37670
Authors: Haocheng Tang, Tianchi Xie, Xingqiao Lin
Abstract: MeanFlow enables efficient few-step generation by predicting interval-average velocities, but this representation creates a mismatch for reward fine-tuning: existing advantage-based objectives are typically defined on instantaneous velocities or equivalent $x_0$-space predictions, whereas inference directly uses the learned average-velocity map. We introduce MeanFlowAdvantage, a signed advantage-weighted least-squares objective for average-velocity generators. Our key construction uses a shared, detached MeanFlow derivative correction to express the reward objective in prediction space while making rollout and reference regularization exact penalties on the average-velocity network deployed at inference. The resulting formulation preserves MeanFlow's native few-step sampler and provides a direct mechanism for transferring reward improvements to the deployed flow map. On SD3.5-Medium, MeanFlowAdvantage improves all eight reported metrics over the matched four-step MeanFlowNFT baseline and, with only four NFEs, matches or exceeds the 40-step DiffusionNFT baseline on six of eight metrics. The same objective also transfers to DNA promoter design, where it supports both teacher-free on-policy RL for a generator defined on a manifold and teacher-guided reward-graded distillation, with the latter yielding the lowest one-step Sei profile MSE among the compared configurations.

### Paper [235]
Title: Proof-Grounded Patient-Specific Clinical Explanations from Knowledge-Graph Reasoning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06549
Authors: Surajit Das
Abstract: Clinical decision support can lack auditable links from observations and encoded knowledge to conclusions and recommendations. Downstream of a frozen, training-free clinical knowledge-graph reasoner, the CKG Clinical Explanation Engine converts patient inference states and disease knowledge into typed facts, explicit rule traces, provenance-linked conclusions, and policy-licensed recommendations. Separate availability, representation completeness, and disease-specific activation distinguish observed zero-activation and partial evidence from unobserved evidence. Language-generation evaluation remains future work. Across six usable workbooks (11,289 patients, 33,867 patient-disease traces, 1,715,928 feature-evidence rows), IG-range validity, knowledge provenance, cross-sheet numerical fidelity (203,202/203,202), and exported fact/report field presence (33,867/33,867) reached 100.00%. Availability-representation consistency reached 99.29% (1,703,811/1,715,928), with 12,117 disagreements across five systematic feature-cohort patterns. Observed zero-activation and partial instances numbered 111,488 and 226,880, respectively. A separate seeded 30-patient end-to-end audit ran failure-free, passing all prespecified trace, licensing, provenance, and state-consistency checks. Paired comparisons (90 traces, fixed inherited diagnostic scores and policy catalogue) showed the availability-only baseline changed every report but no action sets. State collapse changed 20 action sets and removed 10 urgent-review occurrences. Rule replay reproduced all 40 action changes from reclassifying partial evidence as unobserved. The bounded validator detected all 450 single-fault mutants. Structural and implementation auditability were established. Clinical correctness and utility remain unestablished.

### Paper [236]
Title: When to Rethink: Learning Multi-Perspective Self-Verification for Vision-Language Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07018
Authors: Ziquan Zhu, Hanruo Zhu, Si-Yuan Lu, Morris Yu-Chao Huang, Yicheng Lin, Wei Han, Tianlong Chen, Mingyuan Wu, Hanchao Yu, Gaojie Jin, Lu Liu, Bo Sun, Tianjin Huang
Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance in multimodal reasoning, yet they remain prone to generating plausible but incorrect answers. Self-verification offers a practical way to improve answer reliability without relying on external judges, but existing methods typically depend on a single verification criterion or fixed prompt, resulting in incomplete and unstable reliability estimates. We first systematically analyze how verifier capability and prompt design affect verification performance. Our findings show that stronger verifiers provide more reliable judgments, while verification performance is highly sensitive to prompt choice, with no single prompt consistently dominating across tasks. Guided by these findings, we propose \texttt{MOTIVE}, a \textbf{M}ulti-View Self-Verificati\textbf{O}n wi\textbf{T}h Rel\textbf{I}ability-Guided Selecti\textbf{VE} Rethinking framework for reliable multimodal reasoning. \texttt{MOTIVE} evaluates each candidate answer from complementary verification perspectives and learns a correctness-aligned reliability score through correctness-grounded multi-view verification learning. During inference, this score governs an accept-or-rethink decision, allowing reliable answers to be returned directly while uncertain ones trigger history-guided rethinking. Extensive experiments across diverse multimodal benchmarks and VLM backbones demonstrate that \texttt{MOTIVE} consistently outperforms strong self-verification and self-correction baselines. Further results show that reliable verification improves accept-or-rethink decisions and reduces unnecessary reasoning turns, enabling more reliable and efficient self-verification without an external judge.

### Paper [237]
Title: TorchTPU - A Hybrid Eager/Compiled Architecture for Native PyTorch Execution on TPU
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24161
Authors: Claudio Basile, Michael Voznesensky, Gunhyun Park, Ben Wilson, Parker Holloway, Zhanyong Wan, Vlad Belous, Manish Khanna, Paul Ganssle, Kevin Gleason, Andrew Dame, Brendan Folie, Bhavya Bahl, Haifeng Jin, Pedro Goncalves Mokarzel, Arian Arfaian, Luke Boyer, Chi Zhang, Yiling Yuan, Adam Mainz, Aditya Patel, Huangze Tang, Jay Gan, Lizzie Liang, Will Froom, Tushar Agarwal, Karlo Basioli, Theotime Combes, Emilio G. Cota, Minos Papadopoulos, Tudor Pipernea, Yarong Mu, Qi Zhou, Yaoshiang Ho, Aleksey Vlasenko, Alexander Brauckmann, Ziyin Huang, Jack Oh, Jerry Hsu, Tanvi Bajpai, Jialei Chen, Wenjun Gu, Chintan Shah, Chris Chan, Sayce Falk, Chris Jones, Rob Torres, Kat Ko, Lee Howes, Freya Azad, Andi Gavrilescu, Joe Pamer, Bill Jia
Abstract: TorchTPU is an open-source collaboration between Google and Meta that introduces a native and principled PyTorch backend for Google TPUs. It enables seamless out-of-the-box model execution and TPU migration while delivering industry-competitive inference and training performance through eager execution optimizations, torch.compile integration, and native hardware acceleration.

### Paper [238]
Title: FlashRT-PAI: Fast Kernels Are Not Fast Pipelines for Physical AI
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24349
Authors: Liang Su, David Chen, Jason Li, Kevin Guo, Teresa Lv
Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA), video-action (VA), and world action models (WAMs) form the backbone of modern physical AI systems, yet their real-time single-batch inference remains a defining bottleneck. Latency is dominated not by individual kernel computation, but by system-level overhead: thousands of short kernel launches, intermediate data movement across operator boundaries, and redundant recomputation across consecutive decisions. General-purpose graph compilers cannot address these costs, as they optimize individual calls in isolation and lack visibility into cross-call state semantics and global pipeline structure. We present FLASHRT-PAI, a low-latency inference engine built around model-hardware pipeline co-design rather than isolated kernel optimization. FLASHRT-PAI combines three core mechanisms: deep kernel fusion with launch overlap eliminates inter-operator boundaries and launch gaps; an execution contract formalizes the pipeline's stage DAG, update rates, and named buffers to enable cross-decision state reuse and selective refresh; and execution-state capsules enable fast snapshot, restore, and fork of persistent pipeline state. The system also supports drop-in acceleration of unmodified PyTorch models via parity- and latency-gated kernel replacement. Evaluation covers six policies on RTX 5090 and four on Jetson AGX Thor. At matched 16-bit precision, FLASHRT-PAI is 1.3-3.7x faster than official compiled paths. Combining pipeline co-design with reduced precision delivers 3.38-12.66x speedup over official eager implementations. In LIBERO-10, four-bit pi0.5 achieves 94.04% success on Thor and 93.32% on RTX 5090, compared with 92.4% for the measured official FP16 reference. FLASHRT-PAI is open source, distributed via Hugging Face Kernel Hub, and its opt-in pi0.5 kernels have been adopted into the LeRobot framework.

### Paper [239]
Title: Pluralistic Alignment in Large Language Models: Objectives, Human References, and Preference Representations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24471
Authors: Meng-Chen Wu, Umesh Bodhwani
Abstract: Pluralistic alignment aims to represent meaningful variation in human preferences rather than collapse heterogeneous judgments into a single target. Yet existing work often mixes three distinct questions: what pluralistic behavior a model should exhibit, whose preferences define the reference, and what evidence is sufficient to demonstrate success. We organize the literature around a two-layer taxonomy. Layer 1 distinguishes representing a range of human-supported viewpoints (Overton Pluralism), adapting faithfully to a specified perspective or preference condition (Steerable Pluralism), and matching the response distribution observed in a target population (Distributional Pluralism). Layer 2 distinguishes aggregate populations, countries and regions, and subgroups as human references. Preference representation, implementation mechanism, modality, and evidence quality are treated as cross-cutting design choices rather than additional forms of pluralism. We synthesize a protocol-bounded corpus of 70 works on pluralistic alignment and neighboring areas, using the objective-by-human-reference matrix to map existing approaches and identify underexplored combinations. The coded literature concentrates on Steerable Pluralism and Distributional Pluralism, while eligible Overton Pluralism work appears only at the aggregate-population level under our stated protocol. We then connect each objective to the evidence needed to support it, including perspective validity, conditional fidelity, distributional fidelity, support preservation, and uncertainty in subgroup estimates. Together, the taxonomy and evaluation framework clarify what current methods establish, where their claims exceed their evidence, and how future systems can represent disagreement without mistaking variation for faithful representation.

### Paper [240]
Title: TimesJev: Thinking Fast on Time Series with System 1 Sufficiency
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26002
Authors: Chenghao Liu, Wei Fan, Jindong Han, Dongjie Wang, Aniket Mahanti, Pengyang Wang, Hao Liu
Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have recently shown strong potential for time series reasoning, while models that reason over time series are usually with long chains of thought. However, this process often consumes thousands of tokens, directly leading to substantial latency during inference. In contrast, System 1 models such as Jev make decisions instantly and can naturally be an alternative, but raw time series are not compatible with the embedding space of these models, thus hindering the reasoning performance, especially when input series contain thousands of numeric points. In this paper, we make a very early attempt to propose a time series System 1 model, namely TimesJev. Specifically, we first formalize System 1 sufficiency in time series reasoning, which motivates a compressed representation for time series with key frequency patterns preserved. To construct this representation, we derive a dimension reduction objective that combines (i) a sufficiency divergence that measures the core information lost during compression, and (ii) a realizability divergence that quantifies the incompatibility of the System 1 model to the optimal prediction given this representation. We optimize this objective by learning time series compression with a gating mechanism for sufficiency, and then aligning the selected representation with the embedding space for realizability. Finally, experiments on 13 datasets demonstrate that TimesJev outperforms reasoning models by over 62% with 62.9% less inference time than their fine-tuned versions on average.

### Paper [241]
Title: Can LLMs Improve Their Own Harness? A Controlled Study on ARC-AGI-3
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30499
Authors: Jens Lehmann, Sahar Vahdati
Abstract: A generally intelligent system acquires new skills efficiently without requiring humans to redesign its software scaffold for every new task. Yet language models rarely act alone. A harness is a symbolic program that orchestrates calls to the model, handling perception, memory, tool use, recovery, and inference allocation on the model's behalf. ARC-AGI-3 is an interactive benchmark in which agents must explore unfamiliar environments, figure out their goals on the fly, and keep learning as they go. On it, human-steered systems already report ceiling scores across the public games, so we know effective designs exist. We ask whether frontier models can build and improve such a harness on their own. Through a fixed coding bootstrap, each model constructs a complete task harness, acts through that harness on five development games, and revises the entire codebase twice. Every version is evaluated on twenty games that never influence adaptation. We measure task performance with Relative Human Action Efficiency (RHAE) and record dollar-denominated inference cost. Across 27 lineages, harness performance ranges from zero to near-ceiling scores. Two revisions raise mean held-out RHAE by only 2.80 points (95% interval [-1.44,7.35]). Development gains overstate held-out improvement, and reductions in inference cost can come at the expense of task performance. No revision retains its parent, yet a post hoc rule that selects among existing snapshots by development score raises the held-out gain to 4.53 points ([0.81,8.68]), separating the generation of revisions from the decision to deploy them. Human-steered systems demonstrate further headroom. These findings reveal a gap between autonomous harness construction and reliable adaptation under this two-revision protocol: even strong task performance does not ensure that subsequent revisions generalize or preserve existing capabilities.

### Paper [242]
Title: One Offset, Two Directions: Baseline Adjustment via Subgroup Offsets
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00782
Authors: Jiaying Zhou, Jiachen Liu, Qingchao Chen
Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) estimates advantages against the within-prompt mean reward, a baseline shared across the group. In practice, rollouts within a prompt are not always homogeneous, differing in their solution strategies and reasoning trajectories, along which they may group into subgroups. We further find that these heterogeneous subgroups may exhibit distinct reward distributions. The global baseline, however, collapses them onto a single credit axis, where the credit reflects neither a rollout's standing within its subgroup nor the subgroup's standing relative to the rest. We therefore study how to adjust the baseline when the rollouts of a prompt fall into heterogeneous subgroups. We define the \emph{subgroup offset} as the deviation of a subgroup mean from the global mean, and shift the baseline along it by a single signed coefficient $\lambda$. The sign of $\lambda$ yields two opposing directions: \emph{Alignment} ($\lambda{>}0$) adds the offset to the baseline, drawing credit toward a rollout's relative position within its subgroup; \emph{Contrast} ($\lambda{<}0$) subtracts it, drawing a rollout's credit toward the relative quality of the subgroup it belongs to. The adjustment acts on the baseline alone, leaving the reward signal and the optimization objective unchanged. In this work, we induce subgroups from strategies, grouping rollouts that share a strategy into the same subgroup. We evaluate both directions on Qwen3-8B-Base and Olmo3-7B-Base across four competition-level math benchmarks, against a $\lambda{=}0$ anchor that shares the same strategy-conditioned sampling and differs only in the baseline. Against this anchor each direction yields modest but consistent gains in multi-sample coverage, with Contrast the stronger of the two.

### Paper [243]
Title: Function-Preserving Audits of Visual Weight Readers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05798
Authors: Sungguk Cha, Mintae Kim, Youngsub Han, Byoung-Ki Jeon, Sangyeob Lee
Abstract: A neural field can encode the same image with different weights. A reader that recognizes one such representation may therefore rely on parameter coordinates rather than the visual function. We study this distinction through function-preserving transformations, measuring recognition, prediction disagreement, and numerical fidelity together. Across shape, digit, and natural-image fields, high accuracy on the original weights coexists with substantial sensitivity to equivalent representations. On 10,000 official CIFAR10 test origins, one-period hidden-bias shifts change 7.3% of a released HiddenProbe configuration's predictions while barely changing accuracy; three-period shifts change 22.0% and reduce accuracy from 57.8% to 54.4%. Output-only ProbeGen remains unchanged. These effects are smaller than in a separate adapted-reader study, where matched phase and sign controls remove the corresponding sensitivities. Controlled readout studies show why invariance is insufficient: complete canonical hidden features reach 38.7% on MNIST, whereas explicitly composing those activations, outgoing weights, and output bias into field outputs reaches 81.5%. The information is present, but the learned readout does not recover it as effectively. A separate 720-response study of language models reading numerical weights shows why stochastic audits require repeats on both inputs: observed accuracy losses need not yield rejection of response-law equality. Together, these results distinguish invariance, useful visual information, and the evidence required to evaluate stochastic readers.

### Paper [244]
Title: AttentionSwitch: Jailbreak Defense for LLMs via Joint Learning of Sparse Attention-Head Selection and Scaling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08752
Authors: Doniyorkhon Obidov, Honggang Yu, Xiaolong Guo, Kaichen Yang
Abstract: Large language models have achieved remarkable performance across a wide range of tasks, yet they remain susceptible to jailbreak attacks that circumvent safety mechanisms and elicit harmful or unintended outputs. Although safety can be improved through model fine-tuning, such approaches can be computationally expensive and may degrade previously acquired capabilities through catastrophic forgetting. Recent jailbreak defenses have therefore explored targeted interventions on specific model components associated with unsafe behavior. However, existing approaches typically decouple two inherently related decisions: where to intervene and how to modify the selected components. Such sequential optimization may be suboptimal because the optimal intervention location can depend on the intervention applied there, and vice versa. In this work, we introduce AttentionSwitch, the first jailbreak defense to jointly optimize intervention sites and strengths within a frozen LLM. Specifically, AttentionSwitch learns a sparse set of attention heads together with query-conditioned multiplicative scaling factors, allowing the outputs of the selected heads to be either attenuated or amplified without altering the directions of their representations. Across popular language models and jailbreak attacks, AttentionSwitch outperforms existing defenses, with particularly strong results when training data is scarce.

### Paper [245]
Title: ABHBench: Evaluating Moral Decision-Making of Foundation Models from an Agentic Perspective
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09326
Authors: Chaoran Li, Yukun Li, Zeyuan Zhao, Xihuai Wang, Shao Zhang, Ying Wen
Abstract: Foundation models are increasingly deployed as agentic systems that perceive context and act autonomously, raising the need to evaluate their moral behavior as active decision-makers. Existing benchmarks, however, largely ask models to judge human actions from third-person textual vignettes, leaving untested how models behave when they themselves become the acting agent. We introduce ABHBench, a multimodal benchmark built from Detroit: Become Human, an interactive narrative game, to evaluate how foundation models make moral decisions in first-person human-android dilemmas. ABHBench places foundation models in branching narrative scenarios with visual observations, dialogue, memory, and action choices. Inspired by Asimov's Three Laws of Robotics, our framework measures Harmlessness, Obedience, and Self-preservation to capture the core tensions in human-AI relations, along with Autonomy, Stability, and Certainty to assess decision-making patterns. Across 12 foundation models, we find that moral behavior changes substantially under situated agentic context: 75% of models shift significantly between a text-only abstraction and the matched multimodal DBH scenario, and reasoning-enhanced models show no monotonic gains in consistency or human-oriented behavior. These results highlight the necessity of evaluating moral behavior in situated agentic contexts rather than relying on third-person judgment alone.

### Paper [246]
Title: Sage-Taiyi: A Metacognitive Architecture for Scientific Discovery
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12762
Authors: Weida Wang, Benteng Chen, Yigeng Jiang, Shuchen Pu, Weiping Shen, Zichao Liu, Yulin Su, Zitong Wang, Siyu Zhou, Zifu Wang, Yizhi Li, Xin Zong, Yuning Yang, Jixiang Bi, Yuan Wang, Chuyi Peng, Shiyao Geng, Qi Zhang, Kuan Lu, Mengzhu Li, Weiwang Xu, Peishan Xiang, Chengjun Liu, Chengrui Wei, Fangchen Yu, Shengji Tang, Yichen Jiang, Jingqi Ye, Cheng Yang, Tao Hu, Jianbiao Mei, Qinglong Cao, Kun Li, Yixin Chen, Shengyuan Xu, Juncheng Xiao, Yiheng Wang, Ruizhe Chen, Shiyang Feng, Runmin Ma, Xiangchao Yan, Zongsheng Cao, Haomeng Hu, Yu Ke, Taihui Wu, Meng Dai, Xu Guo, Haoyang Peng, Yinglong Ni, Pingsen Shi, Xiaosong Wang, Shuyue Hu, Haijun Li, Sherry Dongqi Bao, Yang Yang, Chao Yang, Jie Hou, Tianfan Fu, Yi Gong, Jin Zeng, Mao Su, Hongxia Hao, Fanyang Mo, Fenghua Ling, Tianshu Yu, Yunqi Cai, Baihong Chi, Yuelu Chen, Jingwei Wang, Lei Wang, Chuncheng Bu, Shide Lv, Guangyu Wang, Yuqiang Li, Chunfeng Song, Lei Bai, Hui Wang, Xujun Fu, Li Cao, Dahua Lin, Xing Xie, Bowen Zhou, Wenlong Zhang, Pinlong Cai, Peng Ye, Aiwen Lei, Jie Li, Shengchun Wang, Bo Zhang, Shufei Zhang
Abstract: We introduce Sage-Taiyi, a metacognition-inspired architecture that uses accumulated scientific understanding to guide what to investigate next and how to investigate it. Through explicit monitoring and control, the system revises its understanding of evidence, assumptions, and unresolved questions, then uses these assessments to adapt research targets and select reusable reasoning methods. On Taiyi-Bench, an AI co-scientist ideation benchmark comprising 265 tasks from 50 papers across ten disciplines, Sage-Taiyi ranks first on all eight metrics of historical rediscovery and open-ended ideation, achieving an Overall score of 54.0 compared with 47.9 for the strongest baseline, AI Scientist-v2. It also performs well on two external scientific ideation benchmarks. Five expert-guided collaborations further connect its proposals to experimental and computational validation across chemistry, materials science, and engineering. In chemistry, reaction-condition optimization raises the yield of cobalt-catalyzed carbonylation from trace amounts to 84% and photocatalytic allylic amination from 13% to 69%. These results support metacognitive regulation as an approach to scientific ideation and its progression toward practical validation.

### Paper [247]
Title: AutoManifold: Agentic Design of Data Visualisation Algorithms via Manifold Embedding
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13710
Authors: Burak Susam, Ruiyuan Kang, Tingting Mu
Abstract: Manifold embedding for data visualisation is dominated by a small set of hand-designed state-of-the-art (SOTA) algorithms. Their design targets are fixed at development time, resulting in inflexible algorithmic behaviour that cannot be readily steered towards user-specified structural priorities. Hand-designing new embedding algorithms to address user priority requires highly specialised expertise and is time consuming. To address autonomous algorithm design tailored to user preference, we introduce an agentic algorithm generation pipeline AutoManifold. It composes new manifold embedding algorithms from a constrained vocabulary of affinity, cost, and optimisation primitives, supported by multi-agent large-language-model (LLM) orchestration. AutoManifold conditions every stage of its design on user-specified structural preservation preferences, and iteratively refines the algorithm configuration through an LLM-guided, metric-grounded iterative loop. We compare the generated algorithms against the three strongest and most frequently used SOTA methods (t-SNE, UMAP, and PaCMAP) on real-world datasets spanning different difficulty regimes under identical evaluation infrastructure. The results demonstrate that LLM-driven, priority-conditioned algorithm synthesis can move beyond hyperparameter tuning, producing genuinely new algorithms that outperform traditional hand-designed methods in their ability to steer towards user-specified properties without compromising the intrinsic structure in the original data. The code of AutoManifold is available at https://github.com/buraksusam/AutoManifold_NeurIPS2026.

### Paper [248]
Title: MIGRO: A Decoupled and Portable Long-Term Memory System for Language Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15315
Authors: Mingxi Zou, Yinghui Xu, Jiaxiang Chen, Langzhang Liang, Wei Zhu, Peng Ye, Runkai Zhao, Ruoxi Jiang, Sijin Chen, Song Wang, Zhuo Wang, Lizhen Qu, Zenglin Xu
Abstract: Language models evolve, but the observations, revisions, and context accumulated through user interactions should remain usable. Each new model must be able to retrieve past evidence, track revisions, and add new observations. We present MIGRO, a decoupled long-term memory system that treats experience as a persistent state shared across successive language models.
  MIGRO follows a simple principle: preserve historical evidence exactly, organize it approximately, and decide what to retrieve only when the question is known. We call this asymmetric fidelity with deferred selectivity. A shared semantic index supports retrieval, while exact records retain observations and their historical relationships. Because models differ in vocabulary and tokenization, host-specific interfaces convert selected evidence into inputs the receiving model can use. Reversible encoding reduces archival storage while enabling exact recovery. The shared state and its access interfaces let successive hosts read and extend the accumulated experience.
  On matched LoCoMo states, MIGRO scores 7.3% higher and uses 99.3% less private memory than the strongest quality baseline. After handoff, continued writing retains 99.9% of full-history rebuilding quality by extending the inherited state. On million-token BEAM histories, MIGRO uses 57.5% less private storage on average than the most compact baseline. MIGRO combines high answer quality, continued memory growth across hosts, and low storage cost.
  Code is available at https://github.com/zOUMINGXI/MIGRO.

### Paper [249]
Title: Small Heads, Strong Decisions: A Controlled Study of Typed-Decision Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15592
Authors: Ritvik Rastogi
Abstract: In September 2026, TypeSafe announced Jev as the first model in its System One category: unstructured state goes in, and typed, calibrated decisions that software can use directly come out. Jev's architecture and training data are unpublished, but many open typed-decision models have followed, including Nimble, the Decision 1.0-3.0 family and Clef. They answer structured questions about a piece of text, such as a yes/no check, a choice among labelled options or a graded score, by reading the text with a language model and scoring the options with a small readout. Each pairs a different readout with a different backbone, training mix and recipe, so it is unclear which design choices matter. We re-implement five readouts and train all of them under one protocol: the same backbone, the same 15 public English training sources, the same recipe with a per-head learning-rate sweep, and the same seed. Our experiments demonstrate that: The complexity of the head does not affect accuracy: on Qwen3-0.6B every readout reaches 81.3-82.0% in-domain accuracy, and a 0.5M-parameter cosine head, which scores each option by its cosine similarity to the question, matches the 27M-parameter joint schema head adapted from Clef in-domain at half its inference time. Reading all of a text's questions from one prompt also matches one prompt per question in accuracy, with 1.6 times faster inference and 2.1-2.5 times faster training on one H100. The backbone's size matters more than either choice: moving from Qwen3-0.6B to Qwen3-4B adds 4 points in-domain and 11 points on held-out sources, while the base, instruct and thinking checkpoints of the same model tie. We recommend spending effort on the backbone and keeping the head minimal.

### Paper [250]
Title: Benchmarking AI risk behaviors
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16318
Authors: Luhan Mikaelson, Robert Adragna, Emmett Brosowsky, Hayley Clatterbuck
Abstract: We evaluate LLM risk propensities across a range of contexts and task demands. We evaluate models for how risk averse or risk tolerant they are in various conditions, as well as how stable and consistent their behavior is across conditions. We present a toolkit for developing model risk profiles that can be used to predict model behavior and which can be adapted to include new evaluations of risk propensities. We also examine how these properties have changed across model generations and families. We observe that 2026 state-of-the-art models tend to be more stable, more consistent, and more risk neutral than older models. These trends appear to be related; newer models are more stable and consistent because they are more likely to use expected value maximization as a decision procedure.

### Paper [251]
Title: S1-Evo: Experience-Guided Evidence Competition Attacks on Typed System-1 Decision Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16937
Authors: Kaixiang Wang, Junjie Li, Zhaojiacheng Zhou, Jiong Lou, Jie Li
Abstract: Typed System-1 (S1) decision models are increasingly deployed in LLM agents for routing, moderation, and authorization to cut inference cost, yet untrusted context can still manipulate typed outputs and alter execution branches. Unlike autoregressive System-2 (S2) models, S1 interfaces score dynamic rules and options without token-by-token decoding, mismatching attacks targeting text continuation or explicit override commands. We propose S1-Evo, a self-evolving framework tailored to S1's contract-based option competition and margin feedback. S1-Evo perturbs untrusted fields via contract-aware evidence channels to induce \emph{evidence competition} with original task evidence while preserving fact invariance. Offline on seed tasks, it optimizes candidates via feedback search guided by directional margins and re-verification, distilling verified flips into experience cards. At test time, S1-Evo retrieves relevant cards to construct perturbations for unseen tasks. Across four benchmarks and three S1 models, S1-Evo reaches 71.99\% mean ASR and 57.47\% high-confidence ASR on \texttt{jev-1.14}, and causes up to 24.00\% E2E agent violations.

### Paper [252]
Title: Ballad: Bandit-Based LLM Routing for Automated Heuristic Discovery
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17241
Authors: Samidha Verma, Ankit Anand, Sayan Ranu
Abstract: Modern automated heuristic discovery (AHD) systems increasingly rival hand-crafted heuristics by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to iteratively mutate and refine solutions. A natural assumption is that more powerful and more expensive models consistently yield better heuristics. We challenge this empirically: across multiple NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems, we find no consistent advantage for larger, costlier models. This raises a deeper question: given a pool of LLMs spanning frontier and freely hosted or open-weight models, how should one adaptively select which model to invoke at each step of the search? The challenge is non-trivial. The optimal LLM may shift over the course of a run, and because one LLM's output seeds the context for subsequent calls, each model's reward signal is non-stationary and entangled across the heuristic population, thus violating the independence assumptions of classical online decision-making settings. We address this through Ballad (Bandit-based LLM Routing for Automated Heuristic Discovery), an online bandit algorithm that learns to orchestrate a pool of LLMs into an ensemble that matches or exceeds what any soloist LLM achieves alone. This orchestration is powered by an LLM selection policy that favors rare, high-scoring heuristics over stable averages, a DAG-based credit propagation mechanism through the genealogy of the heuristic population, and discounting of stale observations to remain attuned to each model's current utility. Across five NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems and a pool of four models of varying capability and cost, Ballad not only matches the best individual LLM in hindsight but, in several cases, surpasses every individual model while reducing API cost by up to 65.6% relative to always using the strongest model.

### Paper [253]
Title: Fake News Detectors are Biased against Texts Generated by Large Language Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.08674
Authors: Jinyan Su, Terry Yue Zhuo, Jonibek Mansurov, Di Wang, Preslav Nakov
Abstract: The spread of fake news has emerged as a critical challenge, undermining trust and posing threats to society. In the era of Large Language Models (LLMs), the capability to generate believable fake content has intensified these concerns. In this study, we present a novel paradigm to evaluate fake news detectors in scenarios involving both human-written and LLM-generated misinformation. Intriguingly, our findings reveal a significant bias in many existing detectors: they are more prone to flagging LLM-generated content as fake news while often misclassifying human-written fake news as genuine. This unexpected bias appears to arise from distinct linguistic patterns inherent to LLM outputs. To address this, we introduce a mitigation strategy that leverages adversarial training with LLM-paraphrased genuine news. The resulting model yielded marked improvements in detection accuracy for both human and LLM-generated news. To further catalyze research in this domain, we release two comprehensive datasets, \texttt{GossipCop++} and \texttt{PolitiFact++}, thus amalgamating human-validated articles with LLM-generated fake and real news.

### Paper [254]
Title: Astraios: Parameter-Efficient Instruction Tuning Code Large Language Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.00788
Authors: Terry Yue Zhuo, Armel Zebaze, Nitchakarn Suppattarachai, Leandro von Werra, Harm de Vries, Qian Liu, Niklas Muennighoff
Abstract: The high cost of full-parameter fine-tuning (FFT) of Large Language Models (LLMs) has led to a series of parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods. However, it remains unclear which methods provide the best cost-performance trade-off at different model scales. We introduce Astraios, a suite of 28 instruction-tuned OctoCoder models using 7 tuning methods and 4 model sizes up to 16 billion parameters. Through investigations across 5 tasks and 8 different datasets encompassing both code comprehension and code generation tasks, we find that FFT generally leads to the best downstream performance across all scales, and PEFT methods differ significantly in their efficacy based on the model scale. LoRA usually offers the most favorable trade-off between cost and performance. Further investigation into the effects of these methods on both model robustness and code security reveals that larger models tend to demonstrate reduced robustness and less security. At last, we explore the relationships among updated parameters, cross-entropy loss, and task performance. We find that the tuning effectiveness observed in small models generalizes well to larger models, and the validation loss in instruction tuning can be a reliable indicator of overall downstream performance.

### Paper [255]
Title: BigCodeBench: Benchmarking Code Generation with Diverse Function Calls and Complex Instructions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.15877
Authors: Terry Yue Zhuo, Minh Chien Vu, Jenny Chim, Han Hu, Wenhao Yu, Ratnadira Widyasari, Imam Nur Bani Yusuf, Haolan Zhan, Junda He, Indraneil Paul, Simon Brunner, Chen Gong, Thong Hoang, Armel Randy Zebaze, Xiaoheng Hong, Wen-Ding Li, Jean Kaddour, Ming Xu, Zhihan Zhang, Prateek Yadav, Naman Jain, Alex Gu, Zhoujun Cheng, Jiawei Liu, Qian Liu, Zijian Wang, Binyuan Hui, Niklas Muennighoff, David Lo, Daniel Fried, Xiaoning Du, Harm de Vries, Leandro Von Werra
Abstract: Task automation has been greatly empowered by the recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) via Python code, where the tasks ranging from software engineering development to general-purpose reasoning. While current benchmarks have shown that LLMs can solve tasks using programs like human developers, the majority of their evaluations are limited to short and self-contained algorithmic tasks or standalone function calls. Solving challenging and practical tasks requires the capability of utilizing diverse function calls as tools to efficiently implement functionalities like data analysis and web development. In addition, using multiple tools to solve a task needs compositional reasoning by accurately understanding complex instructions. Fulfilling both of these characteristics can pose a great challenge for LLMs.To assess how well LLMs can solve challenging and practical tasks via programs, we introduce BigCodeBench, a benchmark that challenges LLMs to invoke multiple function calls as tools from 139 libraries and 7 domains for 1,140 fine-grained tasks. To evaluate LLMs rigorously, each task encompasses 5.6 test cases with an average branch coverage of 99%. In addition, we propose a natural-language-oriented variant of BigCodeBench, BigCodeBench-Instruct, that automatically transforms the original docstrings into short instructions only with essential information. Our extensive evaluation of 60 LLMs shows that LLMs are not yet capable of following complex instructions to use function calls precisely, with scores up to 60%, significantly lower than the human performance of 97%. The results underscore the need for further advancements in this area.

### Paper [256]
Title: Benign Overfitting for Regression with Trained Two-Layer ReLU Networks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.06191
Authors: Junhyung Park, Patrick Bloebaum, Shiva Prasad Kasiviswanathan
Abstract: We study the least-square regression problem with a two-layer fully-connected neural network, with ReLU activation function, trained by gradient flow. Our first result is a generalization result, that requires no assumptions on the underlying regression function or the noise other than that they are bounded. We operate in the neural tangent kernel regime, and our generalization result is developed via a decomposition of the excess risk into estimation and approximation errors, viewing gradient flow as an implicit regularizer. This decomposition in the context of neural networks is a novel perspective of gradient descent, and helps us avoid uniform convergence traps. In this work, we also establish that under the same setting, the trained network overfits to the data. Together, these results, establishes the first result on benign overfitting for finite-width ReLU networks for arbitrary regression functions.

### Paper [257]
Title: LEGAL-UQA: Testing Constitutional Question-Answering Systems for Urdu Speakers with a Parallel English-Urdu Benchmark
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.13013
Authors: Faizan Faisal, Umair Yousaf, Muhammad Hassnain, Zeerak Babar
Abstract: Legal information assistants could help Urdu speakers understand their constitutional rights, but building reliable systems requires test data and evaluation methods suited to the language. We present LEGAL-UQA, a parallel English-Urdu benchmark of 619 question-answer pairs linked to passages from Pakistan's Constitution. Each example includes the question, reference answer, and source passage in both languages. We evaluate two components separately: answering Urdu questions when the source passage is supplied, and retrieving the passage from which each question was generated. We also compare JEV, a decision model used as an automated judge, with human grades. The findings show that model rankings depend on how answer similarity is measured, while a simple word-matching method retrieves source passages more effectively than the embedding methods tested. At the evaluated cutoff, JEV agrees with human grades less often than a baseline that gives every answer full credit. An audit also identifies cases where generated references omit information supported by the source. Together, these findings highlight the need to test retrieval and answering separately, include simple retrieval baselines, and validate automated judges and reference answers with human review when engineering legal QA software for Urdu speakers.

### Paper [258]
Title: AbFlowNet: Optimizing Antibody-Antigen Binding Energy via Diffusion-GFlowNet Fusion
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.12358
Authors: Abrar Rahman Abir, Haz Sameen Shahgir, Md Rownok Zahan Ratul, Md Toki Tahmid, Greg Ver Steeg, Yue Dong
Abstract: Complementarity Determining Regions (CDRs) are critical segments of an antibody that facilitate binding to specific antigens. Current computational methods for CDR design utilize reconstruction losses and do not jointly optimize binding energy, a crucial metric for antibody efficacy. Rather, binding energy optimization is done through computationally expensive Online Reinforcement Learning (RL) pipelines rely heavily on unreliable binding energy estimators. In this paper, we propose AbFlowNet, a novel generative framework that integrates GFlowNet with Diffusion models. By framing each diffusion step as a state in the GFlowNet framework, AbFlowNet jointly optimizes standard diffusion losses and binding energy by directly incorporating energy signals into the training process, thereby unifying diffusion and reward optimization in a single procedure. Experimental results show that AbFlowNet outperforms the base diffusion model by 3.06% in amino acid recovery, 20.40% in geometric reconstruction (RMSD), and 3.60% in binding energy improvement ratio. ABFlowNet also decreases Top-1 total energy and binding energy errors by 24.8% and 38.1% without pseudo-labeling the test dataset or using computationally expensive online RL regimes.

### Paper [259]
Title: Chi-Square Wavelet Graph Neural Networks for Heterogeneous Graph Anomaly Detection
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18934
Authors: Xiping Li, Xiangyu Dong, Xingyi Zhang, Kun Xie, Yuanhao Feng, Bo Wang, Guilin Li, Wuxiong Zeng, Xiujun Shu, Sibo Wang
Abstract: Graph Anomaly Detection (GAD) in heterogeneous networks presents unique challenges due to node and edge heterogeneity. Existing Graph Neural Network (GNN) methods primarily focus on homogeneous GAD and thus fail to address three key issues: (C1) Capturing abnormal signal and rich semantics across diverse meta-paths; (C2) Retaining high-frequency content in HIN dimension alignment; and (C3) Learning effectively from difficult anomaly samples with class imbalance. To overcome these, we propose ChiGAD, a spectral GNN framework based on a novel Chi-Square filter, inspired by the wavelet effectiveness in diverse domains. Specifically, ChiGAD consists of: (1) Multi-Graph Chi-Square Filter, which captures anomalous information via applying dedicated Chi-Square filters to each meta-path graph; (2) Interactive Meta-Graph Convolution, which aligns features while preserving high-frequency information and incorporates heterogeneous messages by a unified Chi-Square Filter; and (3) Contribution-Informed Cross-Entropy Loss, which prioritizes difficult anomalies to address class imbalance. Extensive experiments on public and industrial datasets show that ChiGAD outperforms state-of-the-art models on multiple metrics. Additionally, its homogeneous variant, ChiGNN, excels on seven GAD datasets, validating the effectiveness of Chi-Square filters. Our code is available at https://github.com/HsipingLi/ChiGAD.

### Paper [260]
Title: Graph Your Own Prompt
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23373
Authors: Xi Ding, Lei Wang, Piotr Koniusz, Yongsheng Gao
Abstract: We propose Graph Consistency Regularization (GCR), a novel framework that injects relational graph structures, derived from model predictions, into the learning process to promote class-aware, semantically meaningful feature representations. Functioning as a form of self-prompting, GCR enables the model to refine its internal structure using its own outputs. While deep networks learn rich representations, these often capture noisy inter-class similarities that contradict the model's predicted semantics. GCR addresses this issue by introducing parameter-free Graph Consistency Layers (GCLs) at arbitrary depths. Each GCL builds a batch-level feature similarity graph and aligns it with a global, class-aware masked prediction graph, derived by modulating softmax prediction similarities with intra-class indicators. This alignment enforces that feature-level relationships reflect class-consistent prediction behavior, acting as a semantic regularizer throughout the network. Unlike prior work, GCR introduces a multi-layer, cross-space graph alignment mechanism with adaptive weighting, where layer importance is learned from graph discrepancy magnitudes. This allows the model to prioritize semantically reliable layers and suppress noisy ones, enhancing feature quality without modifying the architecture or training procedure. GCR is model-agnostic, lightweight, and improves semantic structure across various networks and datasets. Experiments show that GCR promotes cleaner feature structure, stronger intra-class cohesion, and improved generalization, offering a new perspective on learning from prediction structure. [Project website](https://darcyddx.github.io/gcr/) [Code](https://github.com/Darcyddx/graph-prompt)

### Paper [261]
Title: BigCodeArena: Unveiling More Reliable Human Preferences in Code Generation via Execution
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.08697
Authors: Terry Yue Zhuo, Xiaolong Jin, Hange Liu, Juyong Jiang, Tianyang Liu, Chen Gong, Bhupesh Bishnoi, Vaisakhi Mishra, Marek Suppa, Noah Ziems, Saiteja Utpala, Ming Xu, Guangyu Song, Kaixin Li, Yuhan Cao, Bo Liu, Zheng Liu, Sabina Abdurakhmanova, Wenhao Yu, Mengzhao Jia, Jihan Yao, Kenneth Hamilton, Kumar Shridhar, Minh Chien Vu, Dingmin Wang, Jiawei Liu, Zijian Wang, Qian Liu, Binyuan Hui, Meg Risdal, Ahsen Khaliq, Atin Sood, Zhenchang Xing, Wasi Uddin Ahmad, John Grundy, David Lo, Banghua Zhu, Xiaoning Du, Torsten Scholak, Leandro von Werra
Abstract: Crowdsourced model evaluation platforms, such as Chatbot Arena, enable real-time evaluation from human perspectives to assess the quality of model responses. In the coding domain, manually examining the quality of LLM-generated content is extremely challenging, as it requires understanding long chunks of raw code and deliberately simulating code execution. To this end, we introduce BigCodeArena, an open human evaluation platform for code generation backed by a comprehensive and on-the-fly execution environment. Built on top of Chatbot Arena, BigCodeArena enables the execution of LLM-generated code and allows humans to interact with the execution process and outcomes. We collected over 14,000 raw code-centric conversation sessions across 10 widely used LLMs, spanning 10 languages and 8 types of execution environments. Among these conversations, we identified more than 4,700 multi-turn samples with pairwise human preferences. Further analysis uncovers underexplored preferences of LLMs in fine-grained domains characterized by tasks, languages, and frameworks. To systematically examine code understanding and generation capabilities of frontier LLMs, we curated two benchmarks based on the collected data, namely BigCodeReward and AutoCodeArena. For BigCodeReward, we post-processed the 4,700 conversations and evaluated the consistency between reward models and human preferences. The evaluation shows that most LLMs have superior performance in judging coding preferences when the execution results are available. Inspired by these findings, we propose AutoCodeArena, an automatic Elo rating benchmark designed to assess the coding quality of LLMs without human involvement. We find that proprietary LLMs like GPT-5, Claude-Sonnet-4, and Claude-Opus-4 still lead in code generation performance among recent emerging models.

### Paper [262]
Title: Manifold Approximation leads to Robust Kernel Alignment
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.22953
Authors: Mohammad Tariqul Islam, Du Liu, Deblina Sarkar
Abstract: Centered kernel alignment (CKA) is a popular metric for comparing representations, determining equivalence of networks, and conducting neuroscience research. However, CKA does not account for the underlying manifold and relies on numerous heuristics that cause it to behave differently at different scales of data. In this work, we propose Manifold-approximated Kernel Alignment (MKA), which incorporates manifold geometry (i.e., local neighborhood) into the alignment task. We derive a theoretical framework for MKA. Our construction removes representation-dependent degree weighting, yields a closed-form alignment score, and gives each row a probabilistic interpretation as a local Markov transition distribution. We further establish kernel- and score-level stability with respect to the neighborhood size. We perform empirical evaluations on synthetic datasets and real-world examples to characterize and compare MKA to its contemporaries. In controlled synthetic experiments, MKA is consistently less sensitive to hyperparameters and captures the selected topology-preserving relationships. On the large-scale benchmark, MKA achieves its strongest aggregate performance in vision, is competitive on graphs, and is weaker than CKA in several NLP evaluations. Our findings suggest that manifold-aware kernel alignment provides a robust foundation for measuring representations and is potentially useful when local neighborhood geometry is important, with possible applications in representation learning.

### Paper [263]
Title: Model-Free Assessment of Simulator Fidelity via Quantile Curves
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.05024
Authors: Garud Iyengar, Yu-Shiou Willy Lin, Kaizheng Wang
Abstract: As generative AI models are increasingly used to simulate real-world systems, quantifying the ``sim-to-real'' gap is critical. For each input setting of interest -- which we call a \emph{scenario}, such as a survey question or operating condition -- the real and simulated systems are associated with unobserved latent population parameters, and their discrepancy varies across scenarios. A fundamental challenge is that, for any given scenario, this discrepancy cannot be observed directly, since both systems are accessible only through finite samples, often of heterogeneous sizes across scenarios. Standard predictive inference methods are therefore ill-suited, as they quantify uncertainty in observable outputs rather than latent population parameters. To address this, we construct confidence sets for these latent parameters and use them to derive a robust proxy for the sim-to-real discrepancy. We then estimate the quantile function of this proxy to obtain a distribution-level risk profile of the simulator, which supports a broad range of statistical summaries, including statistical inference for the real output distribution in a new scenario, the calculation of risk measures like Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR), and principled comparisons across simulators. Our method is model-agnostic and handles general output spaces, such as categorical survey responses and continuous multi-dimensional data. We demonstrate the practical utility of this method by evaluating the alignment of four major LLMs with human populations on the WorldValueBench dataset.

### Paper [264]
Title: NEAT: Neighborhood-Guided, Efficient, Autoregressive Set Transformer for 3D Molecular Generation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.05844
Authors: Daniel Rose, Roxane Axel Jacob, Johannes Kirchmair, Thierry Langer
Abstract: Transformer-based autoregressive models offer an efficient alternative to diffusion- and flow-based approaches for generating 3D molecules. One challenge remains: standard transformer architectures require a sequential ordering of tokens, which is not inherently defined for the atoms in a molecule. Prior works have addressed this by using canonical atom orderings. However, these approaches are not permutation invariant w.r.t. atoms and bias next-token prediction towards ordering conventions. We overcome this limitation by introducing a novel neighborhood-guided training strategy. Our model, NEAT (Neighborhood-Guided, Efficient, Autoregressive Set Transformer) treats molecular graphs as sets of atoms and learns an order-agnostic distribution over admissible tokens at the graph boundary, thereby ensuring atom-level permutation invariance. NEAT achieves state-of-the-art generation quality on the QM9 and GEOM-Drugs datasets, enables seamless completion of molecular prefixes, and offers a significant speed advantage over existing baselines.

### Paper [265]
Title: Vision-language models lag human performance on physical dynamics and intent reasoning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.01547
Authors: Tianjun Gu, Jingyu Gong, Zhizhong Zhang, Yuan Xie, Lizhuang Ma, Xin Tan, Athanasios V. Vasilakos
Abstract: Spatial intelligence is central to embodied cognition, yet contemporary AI systems still struggle to reason about physical interactions in open-world human environments. Despite strong performance on controlled benchmarks, vision-language models often fail to jointly model physical dynamics, reference frames, and the latent human intentions that drive spatial change. We introduce Teleo-Spatial Intelligence (TSI), which captures an important aspect of spatial reasoning: integrating physical dynamics and action goals to reason about actions and their spatial consequences. To evaluate TSI, we present EscherVerse, a large-scale open-world resource built from 11,328 real-world videos, including an 8,000-example benchmark and a 35,963-example instruction-tuning set. Across 27 state-of-the-art vision-language models and an independent analysis of first-pass human responses from 11 annotators, we identify a persistent teleo-spatial reasoning gap: the strongest proprietary model achieves 57.26% overall accuracy, far below first-pass human performance, which ranges from 84.81% to 95.14% with a mean of 90.62%. Fine-tuning on real-world, intent-aware data narrows this gap for open-weight models, but does not close it. EscherVerse provides a diagnostic testbed for purpose-aware spatial reasoning and highlights a critical gap between pattern recognition and human-level understanding in embodied AI.

### Paper [266]
Title: ISLA: Ensemble learning and domain adaptation for generalizable acute ischemic stroke lesion segmentation in diffusion MRI with clinical validation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.08732
Authors: Vincent Roca, Hakim Daouairi, Sevak Yepremian, Hilde Henon, Nicolas Martinage, Laurent Puy, Gr\'egory Kuchcinski, Martin Bretzner, Renaud Lopes
Abstract: Accurate segmentation of acute ischemic stroke (AIS) lesions on MRI is essential for clinical decision-making. Deep learning methods offer a promising approach to automating this task. However, they often generalize poorly to unseen clinical sites, and few AIS lesion segmentation methods have been externally validated. In this study, we propose $\mathit{ISLA}$ (Ischemic Stroke Lesion Analyzer), a framework for AIS lesion segmentation built on the design principles of $\mathit{nnU\text{-}Net}$. $\mathit{ISLA}$ combines attention modules, deep supervision, and ensemble learning across different input resolutions, convolutional block types, and loss functions. We also introduce $\mathit{ISLA\text{-}MT}$, a variant that leverages unlabeled target-domain images through semi-supervised learning. We trained both approaches on the SOOP and ISLES 2022 datasets and evaluated on a held-out internal validation set of 253 cases, as well as on 590 cases from an independent multicenter clinical cohort. We compared them with three state-of-the-art pre-trained models and with $\mathit{nnU\text{-}Net}$ trained on the same data. $\mathit{ISLA}$ outperformed the pre-trained models on the internal validation set, while both $\mathit{ISLA}$ and $\mathit{ISLA\text{-}MT}$ outperformed them on the external cohort. Although $\mathit{nnU\text{-}Net}$ performed slightly better than $\mathit{ISLA}$ internally, both $\mathit{ISLA}$ variants demonstrated better generalization to the external cohort. Furthermore, $\mathit{ISLA\text{-}MT}$ significantly improved performance over $\mathit{ISLA}$ on the external cohort. The proposed models performed particularly well on low-contrast lesions, and qualitative error analyses suggested greater robustness to acquisition artifacts. Both models are fast ($\sim$ 5 s per case), and their code and weights are publicly available at https://github.com/RocaVincent/isla_segmentation.

### Paper [267]
Title: Tracing 3D Anatomy in 2D Strokes: Can Projection-Derived Masks Replace 3D Segmentation for Cervical Spine Fracture Recognition?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.15235
Authors: Fabi Nahian Madhurja, Rusab Sarmun, Muhammad E. H. Chowdhury, Adam Mushtak, Israa Al-Hashimi, Sohaib Bassam Zoghoul
Abstract: Cervical spine fractures require rapid and accurate diagnosis, yet automatic CT interpretation remains challenging as subtle injuries must be assessed across large 3D volumes. We ask whether full 3D vertebra segmentation is necessary for fracture recognition, or whether vertebra masks approximated from 2D projections preserve sufficient diagnostic context. In the proposed multi-stage pipeline, a YOLOv8 detector localizes the cervical spine from multi-view variance projections (3D mean Intersection over Union 94.45%). Multi-label vertebra segmentation is performed with a DenseNet121-Unet on energy-based sagittal and coronal projections (mean Dice 87.86%). The predicted 2D masks are back-projected and fused into approximate 3D masks per vertebra to extract volumes of interest from the original CT, which are analyzed by an ensemble of 2.5D spatio-sequential CNN-Transformer models. On the full RSNA 2022 cohort at native prevalence (2,019 patients, five-fold cross-validation), the pipeline yields vertebra-level and patient-level F1-scores of 68.15% and 82.28%, area under the receiver operating characteristic curve of 91.63% and 90.95%, and area under the precision-recall curve (AUPRC) of 75.64% and 92.00%. With the classifier architecture fixed, replacing 3D-segmentation masks by projection-derived masks changed vertebra-level and patient-level AUPRC by +1.03 and +0.65 points, and the pipeline differed from the reproduced RSNA 2022 winning solution by -0.55 and +0.15 points. No AUPRC difference was statistically significant, and fold-wise 95% confidence intervals excluded vertebra-level losses larger than 1.46 and 2.81 points, respectively. Explainability and interobserver analyses examine reliability. Projection-based mask approximation is therefore a viable proxy for 3D vertebra segmentation in cervical fracture recognition.

### Paper [268]
Title: The Alignment Curse: Modality Alignment Supercharges Audio Attacks via Text Transfer
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02557
Authors: Yupeng Chen, Junchi Yu, Aoxi Liu, Baoyuan Wu, Philip Torr, Adel Bibi
Abstract: Recent advances in end-to-end trained omni-models have substantially improved audio capabilities by strengthening text-audio modality alignment. However, whether such alignment inadvertently facilitates the transfer of safety vulnerabilities across modalities remains underexplored. This question is critical as text-based jailbreak attacks are considerably more mature than audio-based ones; if they transfer systematically, current audio safety evaluations may underestimate risks originating from the text modality. In this paper, we introduce the Alignment Curse, a formally characterized and empirically validated principle showing that stronger modality alignment enables more effective transfer of attacks from text to audio, revealing a fundamental tension between capability and safety. Motivated by this principle, we conduct a comprehensive black-box evaluation of three attack categories on recent omni-models (e.g., Qwen2.5-Omni, Qwen3-Omni): text attacks, text-transferred audio attacks, and audio attacks. We find that text-transferred audio attacks perform comparably to, and often better than, audio-based attacks, exhibiting a clear advantage under audio-only access. This suggests that text-based vulnerabilities play a pivotal role in shaping audio safety risks. Finally, we empirically analyze the relationship between modality alignment and transfer effectiveness across attack methods and models, observing consistent support for the Alignment Curse: tighter modality alignment leads to more effective cross-modality attack transfer.

### Paper [269]
Title: ROCKET: Rapid Optimization via Calibration-guided Knapsack Enhanced Truncation for Efficient Model Compression
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11008
Authors: Ammar Ali, Baher Mohammad, Denis Makhov, Dmitriy Shopkhoev, Magauiya Zhussip, Stamatios Lefkimmiatis
Abstract: We present ROCKET, a training-free model compression method that achieves state-of-the-art performance in comparison with factorization, structured-sparsification and dynamic compression baselines. Operating under a global compression budget, ROCKET comprises two key innovations: First, it formulates layer-wise compression allocation as a multi-choice knapsack problem, selecting the optimal compression level for each layer to minimize total reconstruction error while adhering to a target model size. Second, it introduces a single-step sparse matrix factorization inspired by dictionary learning: using only a small calibration set, it sparsifies weight coefficients based on activation-weights sensitivity and then updates the dictionary in closed form via least squares bypassing iterative optimization, sparse coding, or backpropagation entirely. ROCKET consistently outperforms existing compression approaches across different model architectures at 20-50\% compression rates. Notably, it retains over 90\% of the original model's performance at 30\% compression without any fine-tuning. Moreover, when applying a light fine-tuning phase, recovery is substantially enhanced: for instance, compressing Qwen3-14B to an 8B-parameter model and healing it with just 30 million tokens yields performance nearly on par with the original Qwen3-8B. The code for ROCKET is at github.com/mts-ai/ROCKET/tree/main.

### Paper [270]
Title: ScrapeGraphAI-100k: Dataset for Schema-Constrained LLM Generation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15189
Authors: William Brach, Francesco Zuppichini, Marco Vinciguerra, Lorenzo Padoan
Abstract: Producing output that conforms to a specified JSON schema underlies tool use, structured extraction, and knowledge base construction in modern large language models. Despite this centrality, public datasets for the task remain small, synthetic, or text-only, and rarely pair real page content with the prompts and schemas used in practice. We introduce ScrapeGraphAI-100k, 93,695 schema-constrained extraction events collected via opt-out ScrapeGraphAI telemetry in Q2--Q3 2025, deduplicated and balanced by schema from 9M raw events. The corpus spans 25,453 unique schemas, across 15 named languages, with English covering 89% of detected content. Each instance pairs Markdown-converted page content with a prompt, schema, LLM response, and per-example jsonschema-rs structural conformance labels. We characterize structural diversity across the corpus and identify sharp failure thresholds as schema complexity grows. As a case study, a 1.7B student fine-tuned on this data closely tracks the output distribution of its GPT-5-nano teacher, though it still trails a 30B-A3B reference (3.3B active parameters) on schema compliance. We offer this distillation result as preliminary evidence that grounding schema-constrained generation in real practitioner workloads at scale enables training and benchmarking that prior synthetic or text-only corpora could not support. ScrapeGraphAI-100k is publicly available on HuggingFace.

### Paper [271]
Title: ESENSC: A Polynomial-Time Axiomatic Alternative to SHAP
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00496
Authors: Kazuhiro Hiraki, Shinichi Ishihara, Takumi Kongo, Junnosuke Shino
Abstract: We propose ESENSC, a computationally efficient and theoretically grounded alternative to SHAP. ESENSC is a deterministic, closed-form attribution rule that requires only linearly many coalition-value evaluations, satisfies the null-player property, and admits an axiomatic characterization based on efficiency, a restricted form of differential marginality, and explicit computational constraints.Empirically, on tabular prediction tasks using neural network and XGBoost models, ESENSC shows close agreement with exact SHAP in both attribution magnitude and feature rankings while requiring substantially less computation. Under the evaluated settings, it compares favorably with widely used sampling-based SHAP approximations. While exact SHAP requires exponentially many coalition evaluations, ESENSC scales linearly with the number of features. These results show that ESENSC offers a favorable trade-off between agreement with exact SHAP and computational cost, providing a practical and theoretically grounded alternative for feature attribution in high-dimensional applications.

### Paper [272]
Title: ViroGym: Realistic Large-Scale Benchmarks for Evaluating Viral Proteins
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.06740
Authors: Yichen Zhou, Jonathan Golob, Amir Mohammad Karimi Mamaghan, Stefan Bauer, Patrick Schwab
Abstract: Protein language models (pLMs) have shown strong potential for zero-shot prediction of missense variant effects, yet systematic benchmarking on viral proteins remains limited, a critical gap given the need for proactive tools that can anticipate emerging mutations ahead of experimental validation. Here we introduce ViroGym, a comprehensive benchmark evaluating pLMs across three tasks: 79 deep mutational scanning (DMS) assays covering eukaryotic viruses with 552,065 mutated sequences across 7 phenotypic readouts, 21 influenza neutralisation tasks, and a real-world pandemic prediction task for SARS-CoV-2. We benchmark well-established pLMs on fitness landscapes, antigenic diversity, and pandemic forecasting, and find that the ProGen2 family consistently achieves the strongest performance across all three tasks. Crucially, DMS and neutralisation performance reliably identifies models that generalise to real-world emergence, even though the mutation sets they surface barely overlap, revealing that complementary in vitro benchmarks capture the evolutionary constraints needed for real-world mutation forecasting. The data and code supporting this study are publicly available on GitHub at https://github.com/GSK-AI/viroGym

### Paper [273]
Title: Efficiently Aligning Draft Models via Parameter- and Data-Efficient Adaptation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09527
Authors: Luxi Lin, Zhihang Lin, Zhanpeng Zeng, Yuhao Chen, Qingyu Zhang, Jixiang Luo, Xuelong Li, Rongrong Ji
Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference but suffers from performance degradation when target models are fine-tuned for specific domains. A naive solution is to retrain draft models for every target model, which is costly and inefficient. To address this, we introduce a practical parameter- and data-efficient framework named Efficient Draft Adaptation, abbreviated as EDA, for efficiently adapting draft models. EDA introduces three innovations: (1) a decoupled architecture that utilizes shared and private components to model the shared and target-specific output distributions separately, enabling parameter-efficient adaptation by updating only the lightweight private component; (2) a data regeneration strategy that utilizes the fine-tuned target model to regenerate training data, thereby improving the alignment between training and speculative decoding, leading to higher average acceptance length; (3) a sample selection mechanism that prioritizes high-value data for efficient adaptation. Our experiments show that EDA effectively restores speculative performance on fine-tuned models, achieving superior average acceptance lengths with significantly reduced training costs compared to full retraining. Code is available at https://github.com/Lyn-Lucy/Efficient-Draft-Adaptation.

### Paper [274]
Title: A Variational Latent Equilibrium for Learning in Neuronal Circuits
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09600
Authors: Simon Brandt, Paul Haider, Walter Senn, Federico Benitez, Mihai A. Petrovici
Abstract: Brains remain unrivaled in their ability to recognize and generate complex spatiotemporal patterns. While AI is able to reproduce some of these capabilities, deep learning algorithms remain largely at odds with our current understanding of brain circuitry and dynamics. This is prominently the case for backpropagation through time (BPTT), the go-to algorithm for learning complex temporal dependencies. In this work we propose a general formalism to approximate BPTT in a controlled, biologically plausible manner. Our approach builds on, unifies and extends several previous approaches to local, time-continuous, phase-free spatiotemporal credit assignment based on principles of energy conservation and extremal action. Our starting point is a prospective energy function of neuronal states, from which we calculate real-time error dynamics for time-continuous neuronal networks. With this, as a first contribution we provide a simple and straightforward derivation of the adjoint method dynamical equations, the time-continuous equivalent to BPTT. Importantly, additional approximations are necessary to avoid unphysical temporal non-localities. One such approximation was recently proposed in Generalized Latent Equilibrium (GLE), but enforcing temporal locality of the backpropagating dynamics induces an error in the gain that becomes problematic when processing complex signals. Thus, our second contribution is to design a learning rule for the backward weights to find a fully local (in space and time) set of equations for neuron and synapse dynamics that corrects for this gain difference. With this inclusion of backward weight learning, our theory provides a rigorous framework for spatiotemporal deep learning in the brain, while simultaneously advancing a blueprint for physical circuits capable of carrying out these computations.

### Paper [275]
Title: Comparative Analysis of 3D Convolutional and 2.5D Slice-Conditioned U-Net Architectures for MRI Super-Resolution via Elucidated Diffusion Models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14667
Authors: Hendrik Chiche, Eshaan Gulati, Ludovic Corcos, Logan Rouge
Abstract: Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) super-resolution (SR) methods that computationally enhance low-resolution acquisitions offer a software alternative to higher-field scanners. We study an elucidated diffusion model (EDM) framework for 2x in-plane brain MRI SR and compare two U-Net backbones: (i) a 3D convolutional U-Net that processes volumetric patches and (ii) a 2.5D slice-conditioned U-Net that super-resolves each slice while conditioning on its two neighbouring slices. All models are trained from scratch to convergence on 500 subjects of the NKI cohort of FOMO60K, with a separate 6-subject validation set for checkpoint selection, and evaluated on 70 held-out subjects with subject-level statistics against baselines (EDSR, Swin2SR, Res-SRDiff) trained on the same data. Neither architecture is better overall. On the degradation used in training the two are tied (37.72 dB PSNR for the 3D model and 37.70 dB for the 2.5D model), 0.2 dB ahead of EDSR. When the test degradation changes to k-space truncation, EDSR matches the diffusion models, and with added noise the 2.5D model is the most accurate method. When the test cohort changes to 70 subjects of an external dataset (IXI), the 3D model is the most accurate, 0.4 dB ahead of the best baseline and 0.9 dB ahead of the 2.5D model in foreground PSNR.

### Paper [276]
Title: FlashSampling: Fast and Memory-Efficient Exact Sampling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15854
Authors: Tomas Ruiz, Zhen Qin, Yifan Zhang, Xuyang Shen, Yiran Zhong, Mengdi Wang
Abstract: Sampling from a categorical distribution is mathematically simple, but in large-vocabulary decoding, it often triggers extra memory traffic and extra kernels after the LM head. We present FlashSampling, an exact sampling primitive that fuses sampling into the LM-head matmul and never materializes the logits tensor in HBM. The method is simple: compute logits tile-by-tile on chip, add Gumbel noise, keep only one maximizer per row and per vocabulary tile, and finish with a small reduction over tiles. In tensor-parallel decoding, FlashSampling replaces the all-gather of logits with streaming peer-to-peer writes: This overlaps GPU-to-GPU communication with computation and HBM loads across up to 8 GPUs, with near-ideal scaling at large batch sizes. Our kernel is exact because argmax decomposes over partitions; grouped variants for online and tensor-parallel settings are exact by hierarchical factorization of the categorical distribution. FlashSampling demonstrates kernel-level speedups on decode workloads across 4 different datacenter GPUs (H100, H200, B200, B300), and in end-to-end vLLM experiments, it reduces time per output token by up to $10%$ on the models we test. These results show that exact sampling, with no approximation, can be integrated into the matmul itself, consolidating the bandwidth-bound sampling step in an efficient epilogue.

### Paper [277]
Title: Generative machine learning for protein design
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26378
Authors: Senura Hansaja Wanasekara, Manoj M Wagle, Minh-Duong Nguyen, Xiaochen Liu, Nguyen H. Tran, Manolis Kellis, Ken-Tye Yong
Abstract: Designing proteins with specific biological functions is of considerable interest for both the development of new therapeutics and the engineering of enzymes, yet achieving the intended activity remains challenging. Generative artificial intelligence (AI) has emerged as a powerful tool to explore the vast space of possible designs by learning from protein sequence and structural data. Here, we review 59 generative approaches across six protein design tasks, including sequence generation, backbone generation, structure-conditioned sequence design, joint sequence-structure design, motif scaffolding, and binder and antibody design. We outline common generative approaches and protein representations and discuss how structural constraints and functional information are utilized during generation. This is followed by a discussion of the computational and experimental approaches used to evaluate designed proteins and what such assessments reveal about their properties. Finally, we discuss current limitations in designing proteins beyond well-represented families and incorporating experimental feedback into the design process. We expect that rapid advances in generative modeling will enable more reliable design of a wider range of protein structures and functions over the coming years.

### Paper [278]
Title: Pruning Extensions and Efficiency Trade-Offs for Sustainable Time Series Classification
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07953
Authors: Raphael Fischer, Angus Dempster, Sebastian Buschj\"ager, Matthias Jakobs, Urav Maniar, Geoffrey I. Webb
