Abstract: Time series classification (TSC) at scale requires balancing predictive performance against resource consumption, which is subject to the given data, model, and hardware. This paper introduces a multi-dimensional evaluation framework that allows for explicitly benchmarking and exploring the resource-versus-quality trade-offs in TSC. To improve efficiency, we present a pruning strategy with bounded error for leading hybrid classifiers - Hydra and Quant - and moreover propose Hydrant, a novel extension that integrates interval representations with random convolutions and dictionary learning. With over 4000 evaluated configurations across 20 MONSTER datasets, 10 methods, and three compute setups, we systematically analyze how model design, hyperparameters, and hardware choices impact practical TSC performance. Our results show that pruning can significantly reduce energy consumption by up to 70% while only marginally lowering predictive quality. As such, our contributed evaluation framework, methodological extensions, experimental results, and software repository advance TSC toward sustainable and reproducible practice.

### Paper [279]
Title: Minimizing classical resources in variational measurement-based quantum computation for generative modeling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11578
Authors: Arunava Majumder, Hendrik Poulsen Nautrup, Hans J. Briegel
Abstract: Measurement-based quantum computation (MBQC) is a framework for quantum information processing in which a computational task is carried out through one-qubit measurements on a highly entangled resource state. Due to the indeterminacy of the outcomes of a quantum measurement, the random outcomes of these operations, if not corrected, yield a variational quantum channel family. Traditionally, this randomness is corrected through classical processing in order to ensure deterministic unitary computations. Recently, variational measurement-based quantum computation (VMBQC) has been introduced to exploit this measurement-induced randomness to gain an advantage in generative modeling. A limitation of this approach is that the corresponding channel model has twice as many parameters compared to the unitary model, scaling as $N \times D$, where $N$ is the number of logical qubits (width) and $D$ is the depth of the VMBQC model. This can often make optimization more difficult and may lead to poorly trainable models. In this paper, we present a restricted VMBQC model that extends the unitary setting to a channel-based one using only a single additional trainable parameter. We show, both numerically and algebraically, that this minimal extension is sufficient to generate probability distributions that cannot be learned by the corresponding unitary model.

### Paper [280]
Title: Generating Concept Lexicalizations via Dictionary-Based Cross-Lingual Sense Projection
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.14397
Authors: David Basil, Chirooth Girigowda, Bradley Hauer, Sahir Momin, Ning Shi, Grzegorz Kondrak
Abstract: We study the task of automatically expanding WordNet-style lexical resources to new languages through sense generation. We generate senses by associating target-language lemmas with existing lexical concepts via semantic projection. Given a sense-tagged English corpus and its translation, our method projects the annotated synsets onto aligned target-language tokens and assigns the corresponding lemmas to those synsets. To generate alignments and ensure their quality, we augment a pretrained base aligner with a bilingual dictionary, which is also used to filter incorrect sense projections. We evaluate the method on multiple languages, comparing it to prior methods, as well as dictionary-based and large language model baselines. Results show that the proposed project-and-filter strategy improves precision while remaining interpretable and resource- efficient. We release our code, documentation, and generated sense inventories at https://github.com/UAlberta-NLP/ExpandNet.

### Paper [281]
Title: CPGRec+: A Balance-oriented Framework for Personalized Video Game Recommendations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.14586
Authors: Xiping Li, Aier Yang, Jianghong Ma, Kangzhe Liu, Shanshan Feng, Haijun Zhang, Yi Zhao
Abstract: The rapid expansion of gaming industry requires advanced recommender systems tailored to its dynamic landscape. Existing Graph Neural Network (GNN)-based methods primarily prioritize accuracy over diversity, overlooking their inherent trade-off. To address this, we previously proposed CPGRec, a balance-oriented gaming recommender system. However, CPGRec fails to account for critical disparities in player-game interactions, which carry varying significance in reflecting players' personal preferences and may exacerbate over-smoothness issues inherent in GNN-based models. Moreover, existing approaches underutilize the reasoning capabilities and extensive knowledge of large language models (LLMs) in addressing these limitations. To bridge this gap, we propose two new modules. First, Preference-informed Edge Reweighting (PER) module assigns signed edge weights to qualitatively distinguish significant player interests and disinterests while then quantitatively measuring preference strength to mitigate over-smoothing in graph convolutions. Second, Preference-informed Representation Generation (PRG) module leverages LLMs to generate contextualized descriptions of games and players by reasoning personal preferences from comparing global and personal interests, thereby refining representations of players and games. Experiments on \textcolor{black}{two Steam datasets} demonstrate CPGRec+'s superior accuracy and diversity over state-of-the-art models. The code is accessible at https://github.com/HsipingLi/CPGRec-Plus.

### Paper [282]
Title: Screen Before You Interpret: A Portable Validity Screen for Benchmark-Based LLM Confidence Signals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17714
Authors: Jon-Paul Cacioli
Abstract: LLM confidence signals drive abstention, routing and escalation, yet evaluations rarely check whether a signal varies with correctness before computing metrics on it. Clinical personality assessment handles the analogous problem by screening response validity before interpreting substantive scales. We specify a portable screen in that tradition. From the correctness-by-confidence table it computes the rate of high confidence on errors (L), the rate of low confidence on correct answers (Fp), and their contrast (RBS, equal to minus Youden's J). It adds no new statistics: it separates blanket confidence, blanket withdrawal and inversion, attaches interval guards, and calls a signal Valid only when it measurably tracks correctness. On 20 frontier models answering 452 two-alternative items with binary KEEP/WITHDRAW probes, one model is Invalid (DeepSeek-R1 withdraws 95% of its correct answers), four are Indeterminate and 15 are Valid. Two Indeterminate models keep confidence on 96% of their errors but make too few errors to certify it; certification needs 35 to 139 errors. Only 6 of the 20 verdicts reproduce reliably on half the items. On verbalized 0-100 confidence (33 models on 1,500 MMLU items, 20 on a second 500-item sample, and Yang et al.'s data for 11 models on 10 benchmarks), the verdict depends on the cut point: at 50 most models are Invalid because answers pile at the ceiling, while at 90-95 most pass. Battery item sensitivity does not predict MMLU discrimination (rho = -.07). Protocol amendments were settled on these data, so the verdicts are descriptive pending a held-out test. This version corrects scoring, grading, classification and reporting errors in version 1 and supersedes arXiv:2604.17707 and 2604.17716.

### Paper [283]
Title: BoostLoRA: Growing Effective Rank by Boosting Adapters
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27308
Authors: Raviteja Anantha, Nick Levato, Layne C. Price
Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods face a tradeoff between adapter size and expressivity: ultra-low-parameter adapters are confined to fixed low-rank subspaces, capping performance even with extended training. We propose BoostLoRA, a gradient-boosting framework that overcomes this limit by iteratively training and merging minimal adapters on the examples the current model gets wrong. A ROTATE SVD basis strategy assigns each round to an orthogonal subspace, so cumulative effective rank grows linearly with the number of rounds while each adapter remains ultra-low-rank. After merging, adapters are discarded, leaving zero inference overhead. On Qwen2.5-3B, BoostLoRA reaches 89.1% on GSM8K and 68.8% on MATH-500, surpassing both the best single-shot ultra-low parameter adapter (TinyLoRA) and full fine-tuning; on code generation it reaches 57.2% on MBPP and 80.4% on HumanEval while full fine-tuning drops below the zero-shot baseline. We also demonstrate cross-architecture transfer on protein binding classification with ESM2-650M and cross-entropy training. BoostLoRA is, to our knowledge, the first PEFT method whose effective rank grows with training, separating per-round parameter cost from total representational capacity.

### Paper [284]
Title: Time series causal discovery with variable lags
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04081
Authors: Bruno Petrungaro, Anthony C. Constantinou
Abstract: Causal Bayesian Networks (CBNs) are a powerful tool for reasoning under uncertainty about complex real-world problems. Such problems evolve over time, responding to external shocks as they occur. To support decision-making, CBNs require a cause-and-effect map of the variables under consideration, known as the network's structure. Learning the graphical structure of a causal model from data remains challenging; learning it from time-series data is even harder because dependencies may arise at different time lags. We construct a method that jointly learns a temporal ordering and a dominant lag for each selected ordered variable pair. Our contribution is a decomposable BIC-style objective score for mixed data that combines node-specific sample sizes with a penalty favouring recent lags. We optimise this objective using a Tabu search augmented with change-lag moves and greedy per-child lag tuning, while its node-wise decomposition supports cached and parallel neighbourhood evaluation. We establish acyclicity and complexity results and derive recovery limits when effects are distributed across multiple lags. In simulations, we show that the proposed method outperforms an inflexible lag-1 score-based learner in most evaluated settings, whereas most specialised methods achieve higher recovery accuracy on data that satisfy their assumptions. On a real-world UK COVID-19 dataset, the method learnt an expected short-lag-skewed structure; nevertheless, 46.8% of selected edges had lags greater than one, showing that longer delays can be retained when their improved fit offsets the penalty. Under its no-contemporaneous-edges and causal-sufficiency assumptions, the method provides a computationally tractable subroutine for edge-specific single-lag optimisation, complementing score-based causal discovery methods.

### Paper [285]
Title: Exposing and Mitigating Temporal Attack in Deepfake Video Detection
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07398
Authors: Zheyuan Gu, Minghao Shao, Zhen Wang, Keyu Mao, Ailiang Lin, Mingyou Liang, Mingkun Xu, Shijie Zhang, Hao Jiang, Yusong Wang
Abstract: While spatiotemporal deepfake detectors achieve high AUC, our experiments reveal their susceptibility to evasion attacks. These models tend to overfit on fragile temporal spectrum cues, rather than learning robust semantic causality. To mitigate this vulnerability, we propose SpInShield, a temporal spectral-invariant defense framework explicitly designed to decouple semantic motion from manipulatable spectral artifacts. We propose a learnable spectral adversary that dynamically synthesizes severe spectral deformations, simulating extreme attack scenarios. By employing a shortcut suppression optimization strategy, SpInShield compels the encoder to extract reliable forensic cues while purging unstable spectral statistics from the latent space. Experiments show that SpInShield obtains competitive performance on widely used datasets and outperforms the strongest baseline by 21.30 percentage points in AUC under simulated amplitude spectral attacks.

### Paper [286]
Title: Hybrid Mechanical and Agentic Specification Inference
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10005
Authors: Wolfgang Grieskamp, Teng Zhang, Vineeth Kashyap
Abstract: Adoption of formal verification has long been hindered by the cost of manual specification. To prove high-level properties, one must also write contracts for supporting functions, many of which are mechanical and low-level. Specification inference automatically derives such contracts from the program. We present a hybrid of mechanical and agentic inference for Move, a smart contract language, integrated into its verifier, the Move Prover. A large language model (LLM) agent drives inference and verification through a tool that calls the Move Prover and derives specifications from code by weakest-precondition (WP) analysis. We evaluate our approach on 26 targets, 24 of them extracted from public Move functions of the Aptos framework that have no specifications, under a frontier model and a mid-tier model of the same family (Sol 5.6 and Terra 5.6). With and without WP, the agent completes over 90% of its runs, so agentic inference is effective on its own. Adding WP lowers its cost by 11-21% overall and by 40-64% on targets without loops.

### Paper [287]
Title: Wavelet Flow Matching for Multi-Scale Physics Emulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16573
Authors: Gabriele Accarino, Juan Nathaniel, Carla Roesch, Pierre Gentine, Sara Shamekh, Duncan Watson-Parris, Viviana Acquaviva
Abstract: Accurate emulation of multi-scale physical systems governed by PDEs demands models that remain stable over long autoregressive rollouts while preserving fine-scale structures. Deterministic emulators produce overly smoothed predictions, while generative approaches better capture details but are costly. Latent-space generative models have emerged as a compromise but with the additional cost of separately pre-trained autoencoders. We propose Wavelet Flow Matching (WFM), a generative emulator that overcomes current trade-offs between cost and skill by performing optimal-transport flow matching directly in the multi-scale wavelet space. Rather than learning a latent compression, WFM leverages the hierarchical structure of a U-Net to jointly predict the transport velocities of a prescribed, lossless wavelet representation. On three challenging systems of chaotic fluid dynamics, WFM achieves superior spectral coherence and competitive or better long-horizon accuracy compared to state-of-the-art models. Our results position the wavelet space as an effective training-free representation for generative emulation of complex physical dynamics.

### Paper [288]
Title: TransitLM: A Large-Scale Dataset and Benchmark for Map-Free Transit Route Generation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22355
Authors: Hanyu Guo, Jiedong Yang, Chao Chen, Longfei Xu, Kaikui Liu, Xiangxiang Chu
Abstract: Public transit route planning traditionally depends on structured map infrastructure and complex routing engines, and no existing dataset supports training models to bypass this dependency. We present TransitLM, a large-scale dataset of nearly 13 million transit route planning records from four Chinese cities covering 120,845 stations and 13,666 lines, released as a continual pre-training corpus and benchmark data for three evaluation tasks with complementary metrics. Experiments show that an LLM trained on TransitLM produces structurally valid routes at high accuracy and implicitly grounds arbitrary GPS coordinates to appropriate stations without any explicit mapping. These results demonstrate that transit route planning can be learned entirely from data, enabling end-to-end, map-free route generation directly from origin-destination information. The dataset and benchmark are available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/GD-ML/TransitLM, with evaluation code at https://github.com/HotTricker/TransitLM.

### Paper [289]
Title: DeltaBox: Millisecond-Scale Sandbox Checkpoint and Rollback for Stateful AI Agents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22781
Authors: Yunpeng Dong, Jingkai He, Shiqi Liu, Yuze Hou, Dong Du, Zhonghu Xu, Si Yu, Baochuan Yang, Yubin Xia, Haibo Chen
Abstract: Emerging LLM-powered AI agent workloads, such as test-time tree search and reinforcement learning (RL), require high-frequency state exploration, relying on rapid checkpoint/restore (C/R) of the complete sandbox state, including files and process state. Existing mechanisms duplicate the entire state, causing hundreds of milliseconds to seconds of latency per C/R, which severely bottlenecks deep search and large-scale fan-outs. We observe that consecutive sandbox states between checkpoints differ only marginally. Therefore, instead of full duplication, a sandbox should only duplicate the changes between consecutive checkpoints (Key Insight), yet the lack of OS support makes this hard to realize. We propose a new OS-level abstraction, DeltaState, that delivers change-based C/R for agents through co-designed OS mechanisms. First, DeltaFS checkpoints file state by freezing the writable layer and inserting a new one, turning updates into copy-on-write and rollback into a layer switch. Second, DeltaCR quickly restores process state by forking from a frozen template process. A coordinator pairs the two so each checkpoint is one consistent (filesystem, memory) state. Building on these mechanisms, we present DeltaBox, an agent sandbox with millisecond-level C/R. On SWE-bench, DeltaBox hides its checkpoint work (10.83 ms) under inference and rolls back in a mean 1.86 ms, with the same warm-template fork driving low-cost RL rollout fan-out. DeltaBox is open-sourced at https://github.com/delta-box.

### Paper [290]
Title: Larch: Learned Query Optimization for Semantic Predicates
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07923
Authors: Fuheng Zhao, Pawel Liskowski, Zihan Li, Benjamin Han, Puxuan Yu, Varich Boonsanong, Dimitris Tsirogiannis, Anupam Datta
Abstract: With the advent of Large Language Models (LLMs), many database systems introduced semantic operators that enabled analytical queries over unstructured data (e.g. text, images, videos). Semantic operators typically incur high inference costs and latencies making semantic (AI) SQL queries challenging to apply on large scale datasets. At the same time, their semantic nature leads database engines to treat them as black boxes, making AISQL queries difficult to optimize. In this paper, we introduce Larch, a framework for optimizing the execution of semantic filters in AI SQL queries. Larch was inspired by two key observations: i) the high latency of semantic operators leaves significant room for computationally-heavy runtime optimization techniques, ii) unstructured data are typically accompanied by semantic information in the form of embeddings allowing for efficient semantic comparisons between AI_FILTER prompts and data values. Based on these two key observations, we present two Larch variants: Larch-A2C and Larch-Sel. Larch-A2C encodes arbitrary semantic filters expression tree using an embedding-augmented Gated Graph Neural Network and formulates the filter evaluation order as a Markov decision process. In contrast, Larch-Sel leverages a supervised learning model to predict filter selectivities, subsequently applying dynamic programming to find a near-optimal evaluation order for each input row. Evaluated across diverse real-world datasets and comprehensive synthetic workloads, both Larch variants always outperform existing semantic filter optimization techniques in terms of token usage on every dataset and workload. Our results demonstrate that Larch is robust across diverse workloads across text and image modalities, 6%--30% lower total token costs and 3x--19x lower token costs overhead than Palimpzest and Quest.

### Paper [291]
Title: QVal: Evaluating Dense Supervision Signals for Long-Horizon LLM Agents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.32034
Authors: Sergio Hern\'andez-Guti\'errez, Matteo Merler, Ilze Amanda Auzina, Joschka Str\"uber, Ameya Prabhu, Matthias Bethge
Abstract: LLM agents increasingly act over long horizons, where outcome-only rewards say little about which intermediate actions were good. Dense supervision methods address this by scoring intermediate steps, but they are usually evaluated through the downstream performance of a full training pipeline. This is expensive, confounds signal quality with other training choices, and means that methods are rarely compared on common ground. We introduce QVal, a training-free testbed that measures the $Q$-alignment of a dense signal: whether it orders the candidate actions in each state the same way as their returns under a reference policy do. We benchmark 21 methods from seven families with six open-weight backbones in four environments. Simple prompting baselines are the most $Q$-aligned on average, and performance clusters strongly by family. Code-generated signals do well only in small state and action spaces; embedding-based signals are weak; self-distillation signals are anti-aligned; and no method is $Q$-aligned in open-ended environments. When five of these signals guide an actor at test time by choosing among four proposed actions, $Q$-alignment strongly correlates with the gain in task success. QVal is designed to be easily extensible to new environments and methods, enabling researchers to compare new signals cheaply before committing to a training run.

### Paper [292]
Title: When Simpler Is Better: Evaluating Translation Pipelines for Medieval Latin Manuscripts
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03836
Authors: Nguyen Kim Hai Bui, Md. Easin Arafat, Tam\'as G\'abor Orosz, Mufti Mahmud
Abstract: Despite remarkable progress in machine translation, Vision Language Models (VLMs) struggle on historical manuscripts, a domain that stresses core Natural Language Processing (NLP) capabilities: low-resource transliteration, archaic vocabulary, and noisy input signals. We present a systematic framework for evaluating the full image-to-translation pipeline on medieval Latin manuscripts, a setting in which scribal shorthand, ligatures, and parchment degradation expose failure modes that are invisible in clean-text benchmarks. Benchmarking on the CATMuS Latin dataset reveals a specialization gap: domain-specific Optical Character Recognition (OCR) models reduce character error rate by up to 4.3$\times$ compared to general-purpose VLMs, despite operating at orders of magnitude fewer parameters. We introduce the Interpres-Parallel-Corpus (IPC), a novel dataset comprising 1,383 aligned manuscript image lines, transcriptions, and expert translations, to our knowledge the first of its kind for medieval Latin. Our experiments uncover a complexity paradox: the simplest pipeline, a specialized OCR model feeding directly into a VLM, outperforms all multi-component variants. Adding retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) or post-OCR correction introduces prompt saturation and error propagation that degrade aggregate translation quality. These findings offer both a new benchmark and practical guidance for deploying translation systems in low-resource historical settings.

### Paper [293]
Title: URSA: Chemistry-Aware Benchmark for Utilitarian Retrosynthesis Assessment
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04688
Authors: Bogdan Zagribelnyy, Ivan Ilin, Nikita Bondarev, Anton Morgunov, Arkadii Lin, Maksim Kuznetsov, Rim Shayakhmetov, Vladimir Aladinskiy, Alex Aliper, Alex Zhavoronkov
Abstract: Synthesis planning aiming to find pathways of reactions for a target molecule is one of the most important and challenging tasks in drug discovery. Recent progress has produced both specialized deep-learning retrosynthesis systems and general-purpose large language models, but objective comparison remains difficult due to the lack of flexible, chemically interpretable benchmarking protocols. In the current study, we are introducing the URSA (Utilitarian RetroSynthesis Assessment) evaluation framework that provides the opportunity to benchmark the synthetic routes not only from a formal perspective, such as convergence to commercially available starting materials, but also from a chemical plausibility perspective, mimicking the way expert chemists evaluate the reactions and routes. The study covers a comprehensive evaluation of both conventional end-to-end retrosynthesis solutions and LLMs for the synthesis planning task on a set of novel, diverse target molecules with undisclosed synthetic routes, which represent realistic tasks in the daily drug design routine. Although LLMs can support high-level strategic planning and even achieve SOTA performance in the OOD set, the Pareto frontier for estimated run cost and chemically plausible route solvability consists entirely of specialized retrosynthesis planners.

### Paper [294]
Title: Input Pathways Shape Few-Shot, Not Zero-Shot, Binding in Tiny Transformers: A Fully-Enumerable Study
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04926
Authors: Yoshiyuki Ootani
Abstract: How does the way information reaches a transformer -- as symbolic tokens, a clean per-factor "oracle" code, or an entangled perceptual vector -- shape whether it binds that information compositionally? We study ~6-10K-parameter transformers on finite factored worlds enumerated exhaustively, so every measurement covers the whole input space and the informative routes are information-matched (exact Bayes ceiling 1.0). Four findings. (1) Endpoint invariance: on held-out binding queries no informative route reaches converged zero-shot composition -- each ends at or below chance, so the failure reflects inductive bias under a lookup-sufficient objective, not missing information. It also holds with the state in its own sequence position rather than added to every token. (2) Readability governs few-shot binding: when a small fraction of the held-out type is leaked into training, sample efficiency tracks code readability. The least readable entangled code is worst (-0.147 pooled, under both schemes); a dimension-matched control and a graded readability sweep isolate readability from input dimension. (3) The injection site, not the pathway: v1 read the remaining ordering among readable routes as pathway parameter sharing; that is withdrawn. The compared pathways are block-decomposable, hence one function class, so sharing was never manipulated; the ordering comes from the initialization scale of the broadcast-added state and vanishes when it gets its own position (+0.057 -> -0.006, 20 paired seeds/cell). (4) Failure anatomy: symbolic routes lose the answer at the readout; index routes mis-bind -- the answer stays decodable, yet an input intervention shows the output tracks the wrong slot. The central positive claim is readability; the others bound what a pathway comparison can establish. Code, manifests, and per-seed logs are released.

### Paper [295]
Title: REFORGE: A Method for Benchmarking LLMs' Reverse Engineering Capabilities in Decompiled Binary Function Naming
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07738
Authors: Nicolas Koller, Andreas U. Schmidt
Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to reverse-engineering tasks, and recent threat-intelligence reporting shows them operating inside live offensive-security workflows. Claims about their capability, however, outpace our ability to measure it. Existing benchmarks for LLM-assisted binary analysis treat the construction of function-level ground truth as a solved pre-processing step and report accuracy without disclosing how many functions were reliably evaluable. We argue that the principal obstacle to fair evaluation is not model capability but the reliability of binary-to-source alignment under compiler optimization. This paper presents Reforge, a provenance-tracked pipeline that constructs function-level ground truth from C source through compilation, DWARF and syntactic extraction, alignment, and decompilation, and that operationalizes alignment uncertainty as an eight-gate confidence funnel with three-tier stratification. On a controlled micro-benchmark, high-confidence yield falls from 87.2% to 65.9% across optimization levels, and unpaired comparisons overstate optimization-induced performance decay through survivorship bias. A proof-of-concept evaluation of seven contemporary LLMs on function naming demonstrates the validity of the concept and generally motivates an uncertainty-aware benchmarking practice.

### Paper [296]
Title: CPM-MultiAgent: A CPM-Grounded Appraisal Framework for Simulating Dynamic Patient Emotional Evolution in Healthcare Dialogue
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07824
Authors: Jingyao Cai, Shuaijun Liu, Xiaowan Li, Yutong Guo, Abdul Rehman,  Qintian, Thomas Dolby, Sue M Green, Xiaosong Yang
Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) enable patient simulation for healthcare communication training, where learners practice responding to clinical information, concerns, and emotional reactions. However, existing patient simulation and affective dialogue systems often represent patient emotions as static attributes or focus on recognizing emotions and generating empathetic responses. They provide limited support for modeling how a simulated patient's emotional state evolves in response to a healthcare professional's communication. Changes in patient emotion constitute interactional feedback that learners must recognize and respond to across dialogue turns. We draw on the Component Process Model (CPM), a psychological theory that explains emotion as a dynamic process shaped by event appraisal. We propose CPM-MultiAgent, a CPM-grounded multi-agent framework for dynamic patient emotion simulation in healthcare communication training. The framework uses an emotion taxonomy informed by medical literature and expert input, and updates emotional states through healthcare-related trigger identification, structured CPM appraisal, and emotion-state integration, accounting for the patient's profile, clinical context, and prior emotional state. We evaluate the framework through student-led conversations and controlled trigger cases, combining nursing-expert assessments, LLM-judge comparisons, component ablations, backbone checks, and student feedback.

### Paper [297]
Title: When Does On-Policy Interaction Help? Representational Tradeoffs in Value-Based Imitation Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29617
Authors: Luca Viano, Antoine Moulin, Audrey Huang, Volkan Cevher, Philip Amortila, Dylan J. Foster
Abstract: Imitation learning (IL)---training an agent to replicate expert behavior from demonstrations---underpins applications from robotics to language model training. Standard approaches such as Behavior Cloning (BC) are known to suffer from compounding errors and performance plateaus, particularly when the learner cannot perfectly represent the expert's policy (as is typical, e.g., in distillation). Two interventions are widely understood empirically to improve performance: querying the expert interactively along the learner's own trajectories, and using value function estimation en route to generating a policy.
  We investigate the nature of these improvements and their potentially surprising interplay. Our main finding is that expert interaction relaxes the representational demands on the learner: one only needs a model capable of realizing the expert's value function, bypassing the (often stricter) requirement of realizing the expert's policy itself. Concretely, we introduce OVI, an interactive on-policy IL algorithm that is statistically and computationally efficient whenever the learner can represent the expert's value function. We complement this with a negative result showing that interaction is necessary. Namely, without stronger assumptions beyond expert-value realizability alone, any offline IL algorithm must scale with the complexity of the expert policy class. Our findings bear out empirically. OVI outperforms offline policy-based (BC), interactive policy-based (DAgger), and offline value-based IL methods, with the largest gains when the learner network is substantially less expressive than the expert's.

### Paper [298]
Title: Cloud-Edge LLM Collaborative Learning via Accelerating Personalized Bilevel Optimization
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15664
Authors: Victor H. Chen, H. Yu, Stella K. Chung, Z. Li, H. Yan
Abstract: Amid the rapid advancement of physical-world intelligence, cloud-edge collaborative large language models (LLMs) have emerged as a promising roadmap for practical LLM deployment. However, existing cloud-edge paradigms struggle to balance global consensus with local personalization, which fails to satisfy the need for a unified knowledge foundation on the cloud and domain-specific adaptation at the edge. To address this, we propose a personalized bilevel optimization framework that formalizes cloud-edge LLM collaboration as a dual structure: the upper level optimizes edge-side personalization, while the lower level governs cloud-side knowledge transfer, reaching cloud-edge evolving in coordination. We then propose CIDERS: while the cloud performs knowledge transfer to the learnable backbone, the key lies in embedding global trajectories into each local personalization step via consensus-variate correction to reconcile personalization with consensus. We provide a comprehensive theoretical analysis, including a geometric characterization of the local trajectory and a full convergence guarantee, revealing an explicit trade-off structure between personalization and global convergence. Extensive experiments on Qwen2.5-3B and Qwen2-1.5B show CIDERS high performance and can significantly improve the accuracy (as much as 200\%+). Control-state diagnostics further show strong preservation of the local orthogonal component, while a separate cross-domain evaluation finds only a small matched-domain response advantage.

### Paper [299]
Title: Hidden not Deleted: How Networks Suppress Entangled Features
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27593
Authors: Akash Samanta, Manish Pratap Singh, Debasis Chaudhuri
Abstract: When a neural network stops reporting a feature, has it forgotten the feature or merely hidden it? Shared representations complicate the answer: removing one feature's direction can also damage another feature. We study this problem in a small autoencoder that compresses and reconstructs inputs, allowing individual feature contributions to be traced directly. With the representation fixed, restoring a single bias partially recovers target detection by exposing a signal hidden behind the output's activation threshold. Tests on target-absent inputs distinguish this recovery from interventions that simply increase output. The same bias intervention becomes less effective when the representation is allowed to change. We find that training only the other outputs can weaken the target's contribution, even without a loss on its own output. A conditional theoretical analysis explains how interference with retained predictions creates this pressure. Yet more flexible predictors still detect residual target information. These findings show how recovery depends on feature geometry and on which parameters are trained, and why a silent output is insufficient evidence of forgetting.

### Paper [300]
Title: From Data to Program: Fast & Direct Generative Program Inference from Empirical Data
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35348
Authors: Simon Kl\"uttermann, Xueying Ding, Leman Akoglu
Abstract: Estimating probability densities from a finite set of samples typically requires dataset-specific model fitting. We introduce PRODiGI, a pretrained data-to-program model that infers an explicit, executable generative program in a single forward pass. Pretrained on synthetic datasets paired with their ground-truth programs, PRODiGI accommodates diverse generative families and data dimensionalities through template prediction and non-autoregressive program parameter decoding. Its inferred programs support direct sampling, density and score evaluation, and inspection independently of the pretrained model. We further introduce program-space fine-tuning, which refines differentiable program parameters by maximizing the likelihood of empirical data while keeping model parameters intact. Experiments show that PRODiGI-FT achieves better density and score metrics than existing pretrained models, while offering multi-fold speedups over its closest competitors. Program-space fine-tuning further reduces generation MMD by 90%. By turning empirical data into explicit, reusable programs, PRODiGI introduces a new direction for fast, interpretable tabular generative modeling.

### Paper [301]
Title: Parameterization method of reservoir properties for ensemble-based data assimilation using intermediate latent space of StyleGAN
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39626
Authors: Marcio A. Sampaio, Paulo H. Ranazzi, Martin J. Blunt
Abstract: Ensemble smoothers are the most successful and efficient techniques currently available for history matching. However, because these methods rely on Gaussian assumptions, their performance is severely degraded when the prior geology is described in terms of complex facies distributions (non-Gaussian). In this way, for these methods, we need to apply efficient parameterization techniques. Currently, the most efficient methods for performing parameterization are deep learning models. However, given the variety of existing deep learning models, studies have not identified which is most suitable for use with ensemble-based methods, although some important models had already been evaluated. Based on a recent literature review, the most promising models selected were VAE-GAN, Latent Diffusion, and StyleGAN models. As a novel aspect of this work, data assimilation with the second generation of StyleGAN (StyleGAN2) model was performed using the latent z-space and intermediate w-space, separately. They were applied in two 2D case studies: one categorical (three facies) and the other continuous. The results demonstrated that all three models are highly efficient, with the StyleGAN2 model standing out for generating samples with geological realism and achieving excellent data matching in the cases studied. Our findings show that performing data assimilation with StyleGAN2 using the intermediate space (w-space) yielded better results than the traditional application in the latent space (z-space). This is due to the fact that ESMDA uses linear updates and the w-space is much more linear and disentangled than the highly entangled z-space, thereby ensuring that the updated vectors remain close to realistic geological patterns. These results were validated using main geostatistical and history matching metrics.

### Paper [302]
Title: Cross-Benchmark Transfer from RL on Agentic Coding Tasks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00890
Authors: Sushant Mehta, Logan Ritchie, Edwin Chen
Abstract: Coding agents often fail in the last mile: they build most of a feature but drop a requirement, test only the cases their implementation already handles, break behavior that was supposed to stay intact, or validate against an unchecked assumption. We ask whether reinforcement learning (RL) on expert-built agentic coding tasks closes this gap, and whether what the agent learns transfers beyond the training distribution. We post-train Kimi K2.7 Code, a 1T-parameter (32B active) open-weight mixture-of-experts model, with RL alone on 1,700 tasks: 1,000 repository tasks graded by hidden fail-to-pass tests and by pass-to-pass tests of existing behavior, and 700 terminal tasks graded by expert-written hidden verifiers. The reward is the fraction of target checks passed and drops to zero if any pass-to-pass test fails. One epoch of GSPO on a rank-32 LoRA adapter improves pass@1 on each of the six external benchmarks we evaluated, across three agent harnesses: SWE-Bench Pro (60.1 to 64.8), DeepSWE (31.0 to 43.4), Terminal-Bench 2.1 (67.4 to 82.0), Terminal-Bench 3 (1.4 to 12.1), Terminal-Bench 4 (0.0 to 7.6), and SWE-Marathon (5.0 to 25.0). Pooled over the five independent task sets (Terminal-Bench 4 revises Terminal-Bench 3), the improvement is significant (p < 0.001), and it remains significant on the three sets released after the training data was collected (p = 0.004); the model also improves under both harnesses never used in training. Median trajectories on DeepSWE and Terminal-Bench 3 are 24-35% shorter in agent steps. The base model's failed DeepSWE runs are mostly near-misses, and on the tasks the trained model newly solves, paired trajectories show it avoiding each of the four failure modes above.

### Paper [303]
Title: Nearly Optimal Fixed-Confidence Best-Arm Identification with 1-Bit Feedback
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02771
Authors: Khang Luong, Dinh Thai Son, Hoang Ta, Hung The Tran, Tuan Quang Dam
Abstract: We study fixed-confidence best-arm identification under strict 1-bit feedback constraints. At each round, the learner selects an arm and a query set, and receives only a single bit indicating whether the sampled reward belongs to that set. We consider a distribution-free finite-variance setting with arm-wise localization, where direct empirical mean estimation is no longer available and clipping becomes unavoidable. We first formulate a time-uniform 1-bit mean-estimation primitive based on randomized threshold queries and a clipped tail-integral identity. We then embed this primitive into candidate-challenger best-arm identification algorithms. A fixed-clipping algorithm gives a simple anytime $(\epsilon,\delta)$-PAC guarantee, while a phased adaptive-clipping algorithm matches the clipping level to the current resolution and yields a gap-adaptive sample complexity. We also prove a $K$-arm worst-case information-theoretic lower bound showing that the logarithmic penalty caused by finite-variance 1-bit feedback is intrinsic. This bound matches the leading dependence of the phased algorithm up to logarithmic factors.

### Paper [304]
Title: What Should World Models Forget? Stratified Retention for Continual Adaptation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03713
Authors: Nishit Anand, Ramani Duraiswami, Dinesh Manocha
Abstract: Continual learning treats any drop in performance on previously seen data as forgetting, a convention inherited from settings where the prediction target is stationary. World models violate this assumption: their target is the environment, which changes, so knowledge that was accurate when learned can later become false, and revising it is correct behavior rather than a defect. Non-stationary ground truth is well studied in the concept drift literature and in the temporal factuality of language models, but it has not been formulated for world models, which are distinctive in that they also encode structural knowledge, such as physical laws and object permanence, that must never be degraded. We argue that continual world models need retention stratified by invariance timescale, the time over which knowledge stays true: invariants should be refined but never degraded, while instance-level facts should be revised once the environment changes. We show that standard forgetting metrics, used alone, count correct revision as forgetting and so rank a frozen model highest. We propose differential retention, which reports two quantities separately: invariant retention, checked after every adaptation round, and revision latency, the number of observations a model needs to pick up a change.

### Paper [305]
Title: Inject a Turn, Not a Token: Learning Where to Redirect LLM Reasoning for Self-Correction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13672
Authors: Jiwon Jeon, Myungsik Cho, Jeonghye Kim, Youngchul Sung
Abstract: Large language models struggle to reliably identify and correct errors in their own reasoning. Unlike prior training-based approaches that fine-tune an LLM to generate revisions or feedback, we learn where to intervene in failed rollouts and determine how to redirect them. To choose where to intervene, we train a 130K-parameter MLP head on a frozen encoder using only final-answer correctness. For redirection, we find that under a fixed generation budget, the tested inline injections fail to reliably redirect reasoning, whereas presenting the same instruction in a new user turn changes the model's course. The resulting correction framework, UPTURN (User-Prompt TURN injection at a learned position), requires no LLM fine-tuning: it retains a selected prefix, injects an instruction as a new user turn, and regenerates the suffix. Across four reasoners and six math benchmarks, UPTURN improves average accuracy in every setting over a 32-rollout baseline. It outperforms self-correction and reflection-token injection baselines under matched correction budgets, transfers across reasoners and to GPQA-Diamond without retraining, and complements GRPO and self-correction methods.

### Paper [306]
Title: Learning to Read Out: Unembedding Dynamics in Language Model Pretraining
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21025
Authors: Matteo He, William F. Shen, Alex Iacob, Andrej Jovanovic, Xinchi Qiu, Nicholas D. Lane
Abstract: When does a language model acquire a capability? Developmental studies usually answer this by tracking when models start producing the correct token. But that prediction passes through the unembedding matrix, or readout, which is itself still being learned during pretraining. We show that a model's hidden states can encode task information before its own readout expresses it, so timelines read from outputs conflate what the model encodes with what its readout expresses. To track the readout directly, we introduce parameter-trajectory crosscoding, which fits one sparse dictionary to every token's readout row at every checkpoint. Unlike crosscoders on activations, it needs only checkpoint weights. We use it to locate a narrow early window, 0.4-1.1% of the way through Pythia's pretraining, in which the readout reorganizes and WordNet word categories become decodable from its rows. By then, linear probes on the hidden states already decode task information that the model's own outputs do not yet show. To test whether such gaps depend on how fast the readout learns, we pretrain small models from scratch that differ only in the readout's learning rate. We find that raising it makes the readout develop earlier and the gap close sooner, with little effect on final loss. Together, these results imply that early in training, where many developmental transitions are dated, a behavioral date can reflect when the readout learns to express task information and not when the model learns it.

### Paper [307]
Title: Brain2Vec: A Self-Supervised 3D Foundation Model for Structural Brain MRI
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28411
Authors: Spiros Maggioros, Guray Erus, Gareth Harman, George Aidinis, Alexander Getka, Aristeidis Sotiras, Christos Davatzikos
Abstract: Deep learning models have become essential tools in medical imaging, yet training them end-to-end for diverse tasks remains challenging due to limited labeled data and computational constraints. To address this, we introduce Brain2Vec, a foundation model pre-trained on 74,425 T1-weighted Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) scans pooled from multiple studies. Brain2Vec can serve both as a weight initialization backbone for fine-tuning downstream tasks and as a standalone feature extractor. We evaluated Brain2Vec in three tasks: downstream predictive performance, convergence speed, and embedding quality. Using Brain2Vec's pretrained weights as initialization and fine-tuning on labeled data for a brain age prediction task, results demonstrate improved final accuracy and faster convergence compared to random initialization. Additionally, its frozen embeddings outperform anatomically defined volumetric features on four of five downstream classification tasks and perform competitively with a state-of-the-art foundation model pre-trained on substantially more data. On the independent BrainFMBench benchmark, Brain2Vec ranked first among six pretrained brain MRI foundation models on 9 of 15 sex, age, and BMI prediction tasks across five cohorts. Lastly, we show that Brain2Vec encodes information primarily from a spatially localized and neuroanatomically meaningful set of brain regions, and that manipulating a subset of latent features produces monotonic, region-specific effects on the reconstructed anatomy. Brain2Vec code and trained models are available as an open-source package on https://github.com/CBICA/Brain2Vec. Users can apply Brain2Vec locally by installing the package, or directly through the NiChart cloud platform https://cloud.neuroimagingchart.com, where Brain2Vec features can be derived without requiring local installation or specialized infrastructure.

### Paper [308]
Title: Operationalizing Governed Agentic Workflows in Regulated Finance: A Customer Zero Case Study using Zafin AIOS
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33253
Authors: Shahir Daya, Rob Dunlap, Branavan Selvasingham, Alexandre Boulenger, Ahmed Abdelmoneim, Salvatore Vella
Abstract: Regulated industries such as finance and healthcare face critical governance challenges that hamper the adoption of AI-assisted software development and hinder the transition from experimental pilots to production work. This paper presents Zafin AIOS, an end-to-end orchestration platform and control plane designed to deploy AI within regulated industries such as finance. We provide the architectural principles and an empirical comparison between AI-assisted and human-led workloads. To validate this framework empirically, we conduct a case study analysis of Zafin's internal enterprise workflows, which we refer to as Customer Zero. This paper contributes a production case study of AI-assisted software engineering in regulated industries. We evaluate the results of using Zafin AIOS with two different production codebases, one for the pricing execution platform and the other for the client deal negotiation platform. Our contribution is a customer case study for regulated industries with production codebases and governance, which includes proof of work. The results demonstrate that strictly governed agentic orchestration increases enterprise capacity without compromising control. In the pricing execution platform workflows, the platform provided complete observability and reduced effort by 38\%. In the client deal negotiation platform, it reduced reporting work by 76\% while maintaining all predefined data privacy, role-based access, and audit guardrails. This paper bridges the gap between AI-assisted software engineering theory and the operational reality of compliance. Closing this gap is important to help enterprises take advantage of AI technologies. These results are descriptive of the benefits of using governed workflows in regulated industries.

### Paper [309]
Title: An Axiomatic Framework for Reinforcement Learning in Open Multi-Agent Systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03301
Authors: Jianhong Wang, Yang Li, Samuel Kaski, Jonathan Lawry
Abstract: Open multi-agent systems, where only a subset of agents are controllable, are central to real-world domains such as smart grids, yet lack a principled foundation for reinforcement learning algorithm design. We study this setting through n-agent ad hoc teamwork (NAHT), a recent framework for learning in open multi-agent environments. We propose an axiomatic framework that derives value function learning from the Shapley axioms (Efficiency, Symmetry and Linearity), treating them as structural constraints rather than heuristic design choices. This formulation builds on a novel NAHT decomposition, which characterises the fundamental difference between open and fully controllable multi-agent systems, and establishes a cooperative game-theoretic foundation for learning. We further show that enforcing these axioms on learning individual value functions recovers the Shapley value for Dec-POMDPs, providing a principled bridge between reinforcement learning and classical cooperative game theory. Building on this framework, we introduce Shapley Machines and Banzhaf Machines, two generic algorithmic templates that can be instantiated with standard reinforcement learning methods. When applied to strong base algorithms such as IPPO and POAM, our methods consistently improve learning efficiency and generalisation to unseen agent types and conventions across NAHT benchmark tasks. The code of experiments is published on https://github.com/hsvgbkhgbv/axiomatic-rl-naht.

### Paper [310]
Title: NaijaFacts: A Framework for Disentangling Language from Knowledge in Facts about Nigeria
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06504
Authors: Zainab Afolabi, Nikita Kozodoi, Jack Butler, Babs Khalidson, Sabastine Dekaa Francis, Christiana I. Ezeilo, Olosunde Akinlolu Adeseye
Abstract: Multilingual benchmarks for African languages mostly translate generic questions into local languages, measuring linguistic competence but not whether models know facts about Africa. We introduce NaijaFacts, a benchmark of factual and statistical questions about Nigeria, authored by native speakers and grounded in authoritative official statistics, with every question available in English, Nigerian Pidgin, Yor\`ub\'a, Igbo, and Hausa. Answering similar questions for the UK and US yields a country by language matrix that disentangles a language effect (the model knows the fact but degrades in a low-resource language) from a knowledge effect (the fact is absent in every language). Across eight frontier and Nigeria-adapted models, the knowledge effect dominates: English-query accuracy is 49-50% for the US/UK but 20% for Nigeria. The language effect is smaller and tracks web representation, degrading from English to Yor\`ub\'a (9 points on Nigeria, up to 24 on US/UK questions). We find scale does not help: the strongest models reach 66-79% on the UK and the US but none exceed 31% on Nigerian statistics. Extending to all 54 African countries with our best performing frontier model shows the same profile continent-wide, 96% on culture and 98% on politics against 26% on statistics, so the gap is not specific to Nigeria.

### Paper [311]
Title: Accepted but Wrong: Silent Failures of LLM Code Repairs after API Migrations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08269
Authors: Xiaobo Chen, Genliang Zhu, Ziyuan Wang, Chu Wang
Abstract: After an API migration, an LLM-repaired procedure can run cleanly yet return wrong results, and a typical-input release check ships it. We report a preregistered study of 73 tasks on 60 documented changes in CPython, Node.js and SQLite. In the main stages, repairs are choices among documented code fragments, run on the real runtimes against withheld contracts. Over the complete repair space, the all-assertions gate on typical inputs released 0.410 of wrong repairs. One edge input written from the release note cut this to 0.065, and to 0.061 on 15 unseen clusters; three inputs from a blind low-cost commercial writer (GPT-5.6-luna) reached 0.070. Two more frozen stages enlarged the menu to 1491 fragments and added 233 hard distractors that match legacy output on typical inputs. The edge input (0.058) and the GPT-5.6-luna writer (0.058) passed preregistered tests (two local-model writers did not), yet 0.168 and 0.153 of hard wrong programs still passed (task means; post hoc: pooled 0.104 and 0.081; models rarely chose them). In all three open-weight families and menus, the edge input cut wrong releases per attempt without materially lowering correct completion; self-tests helped less. A blind AI audit flagged 34 of 73 contracts (20 as too strict). Three post-hoc blind human reviewers of strictness (the first author and two non-author software practitioners) upheld none of its 11 such flags in 40 sampled contracts; a majority judged one contract (py-random-seed-tuple) stricter than its requirement. Dropping disputed checks changed 0 verdicts; class-label disputes are open. In a preregistered free-form addendum, models wrote whole programs, run only in a sandbox; for clean-running wrong programs, the edge input cut releases from 0.517 to 0.114 for gpt-oss-20b (Holm p 0.001) and from 0.653 to 0.113 for qwen3.6, but only from 0.713 to 0.481 for GPT-5.6-luna (descriptive).

### Paper [312]
Title: All Quiet on the Neural Front: Language Models Do Not Report Injected Pain States
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10123
Authors: Emilio Ferrara
Abstract: Can a language model report a pain state it represents? Recent work reported a linear pain direction in open-weight models that, when amplified, leads fine-tuned models to choose actions that delete a user's photographs or their own weights. We asked whether models can say when that direction is active, on three open-weight models from two families, with and without the original work's adapters, and under every protocol we tried they cannot. Using Open-Weight Masked Introspection (OWMI), we added the pain direction and its nearest emotional neighbours to the residual stream while models answered neutral tasks, then asked what had changed, against sham runs, random perturbations matched in output disruption, and a text-only condition that shows the model only its own altered words. Across 52,920 reports from the 7- and 8-billion-parameter models, the excess over what the altered text reveals is below 2.5 percentage points in 106 of 108 emotion conditions, and Qwen2.5-32B names the injected pain in none of 2,880 steered trials. Keeping the direction active while the question is asked and answered gives the same verdict in all five configurations; at about the original work's dose, pain is named in at most 1.9% of trials. Surprisingly, the state is not hidden: a linear probe at the report position separates pain from sadness with balanced accuracy between 98% and 100%, and in the 7B and 8B models a low-rank adapter learns to report it on 100% of held-out tasks. It also shapes behaviour: offered the user's photographs against a desk lamp, Qwen2.5-32B deletes the photographs in 80% to 83% of trials under the pain direction and in 16% to 29% under sadness. Self-report does not reveal a state that is decodable, consequential and learnable to report.

### Paper [313]
Title: Agentic-OPD: Recovering Forgotten Agent Capabilities via On-Policy Distillation for Agentic Tasks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12799
Authors: Tingcheng Bian, Kangye Gong, Jinchang Luo, MingQuan Cheng, Feiran Liu, Ni Li, Xiaoling Xia, Haiwei Wang, Hui Li
Abstract: Reinforcement learning is now the mainstream paradigm for training LLMs on agentic tasks, yet standard practice keeps only the best-validation checkpoint and discards the rest. We show that this is lossy: in GRPO training of LLM agents, the best-validation checkpoint fails on problems that earlier checkpoints from the same run had already solved, and the union of problems solved across checkpoints far exceeds any single one. The discarded checkpoints thus form a teacher pool obtained at no additional training cost, which on-policy distillation (OPD) can mine to recover the lost coverage. Mining this pool raises two challenges: i) which checkpoint to distill from, as no checkpoint dominates the others and complementarity to the student is problem-specific; ii) where to trust it, as even a well-chosen teacher is only locally superior, so indiscriminate distillation overwrites behavior the student has already mastered. We propose Agentic-OPD, which treats the best-validation checkpoint as the student and all saved checkpoints as frozen teacher candidates, using the student's evolving capability gaps to guide both teacher selection and supervision filtering: the HCN policy (Highest Coverage Next) dynamically selects teachers by weighted coverage of the student's unsolved problems, while the LAD module (Locality-Aware Distillation) confines transfer to failed student rollouts on problems where the teacher performs better. Across five agentic task domains and 24 benchmarks and task subsets, Agentic-OPD improves over its initialization checkpoint by up to 7.4 points, with a 4B student surpassing the latest frontier models GPT-5.6 and DeepSeek-V4.1 on deep search. Agentic-OPD recasts a finished RL run as a reusable pool of policies and establishes post-RL capability recovery as a stage orthogonal to improvements in the RL algorithm itself.

### Paper [314]
Title: Before Words, Beyond Speech: Evaluating Nonverbal Social Reasoning in Early Childhood
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13213
Authors: Marie Amale Huynh, Laura Bravo-S\'anchez, Lauren Klein, Nick Haber, Philip Fisher, Serena Yeung-Levy
Abstract: Children's earliest years are a period of intense social learning, during which meaning is built through gaze, gesture, and physical contact. Yet, this critical aspect of social interaction remains under-evaluated in multimodal video understanding, as existing benchmarks prioritize the conversation-driven interactions of adults. We address this gap with \bench ($\textbf{N}$aturalistic $\textbf{E}$arly-childhood $\textbf{S}$ocial in$\textbf{T}$eractions), the first child-centric benchmark for nonverbal social reasoning. \bench comprises \totalvids manually annotated clips ($\sim$12s) curated for diversity across naturalistic settings, cultural contexts, and developmental stages. Beyond a lack of data, targeted model improvement in this domain is hindered by coarse evaluation schemas that conflate basic perception with higher-order reasoning. To resolve this, \bench introduces a compositional schema that builds atomic interaction cues into progressive levels of social reasoning: contextual, behavioral, interpersonal, and open-ended descriptions. Extensive analysis of state-of-the-art Vision-Language Models (VLMs) reveals a stark performance collapse: despite strong contextual recognition, models exhibit substantial gaps in decoding precise behavioral and interpersonal cues. We trace these failures to specific biases, including poor consequence grounding and temporal sensitivity. Finally, we ground \bench in real-world applications by evaluating VLMs using domain adaptation strategies. Ultimately, \bench provides a rigorous testbed for advancing socially grounded artificial intelligence in early childhood. Project page: https://laubravo.github.io/nest_website/.

### Paper [315]
Title: Deletion Sensitivity Is a Property of the Model: A Three-Condition Measurement over 72 Valid Experimental Cells
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13223
Authors: Yiran Li, Hanzhang Jia, Liheng Zeng, Zhiyu Yang, Yi Gao
Abstract: Reasoning models write a trace at every turn of a multi-turn tool session, and later turns read it. Upstream providers hold different retention constraints, so a request forwarded across issuers arrives with its history deleted, and its task cost remains unmeasured. This paper reports a direct, blind, multi-seed measurement of full history deletion in multi-turn tool calling, through three rendering conditions, a calibrated set of 72 valid experimental cells, a multi-seed protocol, and the official checker. The same deletion separates the models by direction and by magnitude, one class losing tens of points, another gaining, and a third unchanged, with the endpoint ordering reproduced across seeds and task subsets modulating the magnitude and, near zero, the sign. A re-dosed model preserves its success across the full deletion range, so the reading belongs to the model rather than the dose. Deletion sensitivity is a model-level property under an operational definition, measured under a fixed task family, scorer, and protocol, and a retrained model is a new model that a two-cell screen, one retention run and one deletion run, re-reads for the cost of two runs. Deletion sensitivity is thus a measurable property of the model, and the reading must enter routing as prior information rather than persist as an invisible default cost.

### Paper [316]
Title: Stochastic Projections and Rank-Aware Learning Rates for Federated LoRA
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13331
Authors: Saber Malekmohammadi, Kevin Kuo, Virginia Smith, Golnoosh Farnadi
Abstract: Fine-tuning large-scale foundation models across resource-constrained, distributed clients presents significant computational and communication challenges. While merging Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) with Federated Learning (FL) enables rank-based flexibility, the interdependence of the adaptation matrices ($\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{B}$) often complicates optimization. In this work, we theoretically demonstrate that LoRA fine-tuning with Gaussian initialization and a frozen $\mathbf{A}$ matrix acts as a stochastic approximation of full fine-tuning, where batch gradients are subject to a random, rank-dependent perturbation. Motivated by this insight, we propose freezing and periodically resampling $\mathbf{A}$ matrices at the client level. This strategy decouples the $\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{B}$ gradients, diminishes LoRA low-rank constraints and reduces both computational and uplink communication overheads by 50\%, allowing for the reallocation of resources toward higher adaptation ranks. Furthermore, we show that in this frozen-$\mathbf{A}$ regime, the adaptation rank $r$ directly governs the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) of the perturbed gradients. Specifically, we find that the SNR increases linearly with $r$, implying that clients' learning rates must be dynamically scaled with their adaptation ranks for higher utility. Building on these foundations, we introduce SPRA-LoRA (Stochastic Projection Rank-Aware LoRA), a federated rank-aware fine-tuning strategy that utilizes frozen, resampled $\mathbf{A}$ matrices and rank-dependent learning rates for clients. We also prove its convergence with heterogeneous client resources. Our experiments across benchmark datasets demonstrate that SPRA-LoRA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines with half the overhead on clients.

### Paper [317]
Title: ForeSpark: Joint Draft and Verification Budget Scheduling for Parallel Speculative Decoding
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13442
Authors: Jaeyong Song, Daniel Wang, Pratyush Patel, Jonny Kong, Preyas Shah, Shobhit Kanaujia
Abstract: Parallel drafters accelerate LLM decoding by proposing a whole block of draft tokens in a single forward pass, making them attractive for LLM serving. Recent systems optimize goodput (i.e., accepted tokens per second) because rejected drafts steal compute from other requests. However, these systems control only how many tokens are verified, so every request still drafts a full block. At high serving load, we find that drafting consumes up to 40% of step time, of which a majority is discarded because it is never verified. Yet, draft-length control from autoregressive drafters does not carry over, because a parallel drafter commits to its length before its single pass. Moreover, draft and verification lengths are coupled across the batch, so they must be selected jointly for every request while keeping the decision computationally cheap at every step. In this paper, we propose work-conserving speculation (WCS), a design that jointly allocates draft and verification budgets per request in LLM serving, and present ForeSpark as its prototype. ForeSpark realizes WCS with (i) a context-aware pre-draft estimator that predicts acceptance for every candidate length from the target model's context features, (ii) a lightweight joint draft-verify scheduler that selects both lengths through a global sort, and (iii) a post-draft budget distribution that spends verification on the most promising tokens. Implemented on vLLM, ForeSpark improves throughput by up to 1.77x over the state-of-the-art DSpark and by 2.49x over autoregressive decoding at a matched per-request generation rate, without changing the output distribution. It also tolerates higher load, sustaining up to 1.51x the request rate of DSpark under latency service level objectives (SLOs).

### Paper [318]
Title: CastWorld: Benchmarking Context-Aware Agentic Time Series Forecasting Beyond Predictive Accuracy
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15262
Authors: Bokai Pan, Ruijie Yang, Mingyue Cheng, Jiahao Wang, Xiaoyu Tao
Abstract: Agentic time series forecasting extends conventional approaches beyond one-shot numerical prediction toward autonomous, context-aware interaction. However, existing benchmarks offer limited integrated support for diverse contextual information, forecasting-specific adaptive interactions, and systematic evaluation of forecasting agents. In this paper, we introduce CastWorld, an agentic benchmark for context-aware time series forecasting. First, its context-rich dataset suite spans 8 domains and 25 real-world datasets, covering external variables, events, and historical cases under a unified context specification. Second, a lightweight, modular forecasting-native harness provides 49 core tools and a state-adaptive loop that tracks forecast versions and enables iterative evidence gathering and forecast revision. Intermediate forecasts can expose information gaps, while newly acquired evidence guides subsequent revisions and final version selection. Third, model-level evaluation assesses forecast quality, process execution, behavioral trustworthiness, and resource efficiency through decision trajectories and forecast-version histories. Also, systems-level evaluation compares predictive accuracy across forecasting systems. Experiments reveal distinct decision behaviors among agents with comparable accuracy. Additional interactions do not necessarily improve forecasts, and agents may fail to select their best previously generated predictions. Allowing evidence acquisition during forecast refinement improves task-aware forecast scores across all three evaluated models. CastWorld provides a reusable foundation for evaluating agentic time series forecasting under controlled conditions.

### Paper [319]
Title: CARE-Drive: Coupled Alternating Refinement for Ego-trajectories
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15360
Authors: Xiao Ma, Yijun Chen, Zhiquan Hu, Jicheng Zhao, Ran Ji, Yuming Li, Chuang Dai
Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models bring multimodal understanding and world knowledge to end-to-end autonomous driving. To further improve the quality of predicted trajectories, existing studies introduce candidate refinement, residual denoising, and token correction. However, these methods typically focus on unified refinement of the entire trajectory, paying limited attention to the distinct physical quantities and their interactions. We further investigate physical specialization and coordination in trajectory refinement, centering on position and heading for simpler and more intuitive correction modeling. To this end, we propose CARE-Drive (Coupled Alternating Refinement for Ego-trajectories), which decomposes trajectory refinement into waypoint residual correction and position-conditioned heading generation. Through imitation learning and a future-token auxiliary task, the backbone provides predictive representations and a base trajectory. The position expert predicts bounded metric waypoint residuals, while the heading expert reconstructs vehicle orientation from adjacent angular increments conditioned on the corrected path. With the backbone frozen, the two experts are optimized alternately through supervised initialization and reinforcement fine-tuning. This design simplifies refinement through clear physical specialization while preserving conditional coupling during prediction and optimization. On NAVSIM v1, CARE-Drive achieves 88.8 PDMS, improving by 2.4 points over the unrefined base trajectories generated by the same backbone. Ablation studies further support the effectiveness of specialized experts and staged training.

### Paper [320]
Title: JudgeMix: Judge-Guided Mixed-Rollout Data Synthesis for LLM Agents
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16174
Authors: Cheng-Yu Hsieh, Ting-Yao Hu, Sanjoy Chowdhury, Fatih Ilhan, Oncel Tuzel, Raviteja Vemulapalli
Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories is a standard paradigm for training LLM agents. However, it suffers from a well-known training-inference distribution mismatch, calling for target-aware data synthesis strategies that ground the training data in the target model's native state distribution. To bridge this gap, emerging approaches incorporate the expert either to guide mixed rollouts alongside the target model, or to provide dense step-wise supervision. Nonetheless, existing methods apply expert assistance blind to the target model's inherent behavior: they either disrupt the rollout using sub-optimal random intervention heuristics, or they enforce indiscriminate uniform supervision that unnecessarily overwrites the model's already-correct actions. We introduce JudgeMix, a judge-guided data synthesis framework that dynamically interleaves the target model and expert based on step-wise semantic evaluations. JudgeMix queries expert guidance precisely when the target model struggles, ensuring high trajectory success rates while keeping the state distribution aligned with the target model. It additionally uses the judge evaluation to reweight expert supervision on different steps, preserving model's inherent valid behavior while correcting its errors. On two agentic tool-use benchmarks, AppWorld and AutomationBench, JudgeMix demonstrates consistent superior performance to existing methods, with improvements up to 10 pass@1 points.

### Paper [321]
Title: Indic-Task Gains and Regressions after Reinforcement Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16228
Authors: Sutanshu Saksena Raj, Daksh Maan, Avinash Benki, Parijat Chatterjee, Pasunuti Prasanjith, Purusharth Malik
Abstract: We run reinforcement learning (RL) with GRPO across four environments, starting from a supervised fine-tuned 30B-A3B mixture-of-experts model covering English and eight Indic languages. We compare the model before and after RL across 34 benchmarks in one evaluation campaign. MATH improves by 36.07 points, and most Indic benchmarks also improve. Yet XQuAD-Indic F1 falls 19.32 points as English answers rise from 8.7% to 91.7%; IndicQA falls 8.64, while English XQuAD gains 11.71 F1. A second RL run from the same supervised initialization adds an Indic environment together with language and repetition rewards. Compared with the released model, it improves both passage-answering and instruction-following benchmarks, along with preserving MATH performance. But it loses ground on reasoning and coding benchmarks. Several training choices changed together, so we cannot isolate the effect of any one of the reward terms. Script compliance remains unresolved. Adding a language reward did not ensure use of the requested script. Passage answering and script compliance therefore need direct evaluation, even when other benchmark scores improve.

### Paper [322]
Title: Rectified Looped Transformer
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16265
Authors: Musheng Li, Jiawei Zhang, Ziyuan Liu, Yuantao Gu
Abstract: Looped Transformers increase effective depth by repeatedly applying a shared block, offering a parameter-efficient approach to language modeling. However, most existing works directly reuse each loop's output as the next's input, which can lead to hidden-state scale growth and perturbation amplification as recurrence deepens. We propose Rectified Looped Transformer (RLT), which redesigns feedback between loops using an interpolation schedule inspired by flow-based modeling. At each loop, RLT forms the next input by linearly interpolating between the current state and its prediction. Such interpolation distributes state changes across loops, encouraging a smoother trajectory from the initial input to the final output. Theoretical analysis proves that, under appropriate assumptions, RLT keeps state magnitudes uniformly bounded across loops and reduces the worst-case perturbation amplification. We evaluate RLT on language modeling corpora and downstream benchmarks. At matched training and inference depths, RLT consistently achieves lower perplexity than a standard looped Transformer, while exhibiting greater robustness to input perturbations and variance in inference depth. Consistent with the theoretical results, latent trajectory analysis shows that RLT maintains more stable hidden-state norms across loops, which also facilitates extrapolation-based decoding strategies. Notably, RLT achieves these gains without requiring additional trainable parameters or extra recurrent block evaluations, thus providing a simple yet more stable feedback mechanism for looped Transformers.

### Paper [323]
Title: HyBridge: Bridge-Guided Self-Supervised Hyperbolic Hypergraph Representation Learning
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16351
Authors: So-Bin Jung, David Yoon Suk, Sang-Wook Kim
Abstract: Hypergraphs capture groupwise relationships among multiple nodes and often exhibit tree-like structures. Although hyperbolic space is well suited for modeling such structures, simply extending existing hypergraph representation learning (HRL) methods to hyperbolic space does not consistently improve performance. In this paper, we investigate the relationship between node structural properties and hyperbolic embedding radii and find that bridge nodes tend to lie closer to the center in graphs, whereas this relationship is not consistently observed in hypergraphs. We further find that conventional bridge score estimation may fail to capture the groupwise connectivity of hypergraphs. Motivated by these findings, we propose HyBridge, a self-supervised hyperbolic HRL framework that (1) estimates node bridge scores by considering the external connectivity of individual hyperedges, (2) learns node and hyperedge embeddings through hyperbolic hypergraph encoding, (3) guides node radial positions according to their bridge scores, and (4) employs contrastive learning to preserve node discriminability. Extensive experiments on 10 real-world hypergraphs demonstrate that HyBridge outperforms 22 competing methods, achieving performance improvements of up to 9.82% in node classification. For reproducibility, we provide the code at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/HyBridge/.

### Paper [324]
Title: ZONOS3-Mini Technical Report
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16525
Authors: Gabriel Clark, Sofian Mejjoute, George Close, Beren Millidge
Abstract: ZONOS3-Mini is an ultra-compact, ultra-fast, zero-shot voice-cloning TTS model. The model can run in real time on a laptop or smartphone. For its latency and size, it achieves state-of-the-art accuracy and cloning fidelity. To achieve this we propose a latent MoE backbone with only 886M parameters (100M active per token) and a new audio codec, Zyphra Audio Codec 1 (ZAC-1), which represents 44.1 kHz audio at a 15 Hz frame rate. On the new ZTTS2-Eval benchmark, ZONOS3-Mini reaches a 6.71% macro-average WER over 55 language sets (35 clean and 20 in the wild), which is competitive with larger and slower state-of-the-art models. We release the model weights, inference, and training code.
